The selector is `html body a#back-to-home` so it outranks each page's own `!important` phone rules and the inline style.
- **GARDEN TIPS ON AN IPHONE.** It worked in every emulation and not on the phone. The heading sat about 20px above Safari's floating toolbar, which takes taps that close for itself, and it was a plain heading. It is lifted to `bottom: calc(64px + safe area)` and given `role="button"`, `tabindex`, and a `touchend` toggle that ignores the click the phone fires after it. **Anything tappable near the bottom of a phone screen needs the same clearance.**
- **The bouquet reveal reads: bouquet, an "Edit bouquet" link, then Share and Send to a friend at their own width, then Start over as a text link.** Nothing stretches across the screen. Edit returns to the last studio tab.
- **Garden Tips OPENS AS A CARD on a phone**, a cream panel with a shadow, since opened on the grass its list ran across the flowers unreadably.
- **Titles no longer disappear under the corner icons on a phone.** Once a page has scrolled, `garden-cursor.js` sets `data-gg-scrolled` and a frosted strip sits behind the icons, so copy passes under a band. The gardens' step screens scroll inside fixed `.gg-wrap`s, which now start at 58px, below the icon row. **Home's colour is pinned** to the icons' dark teal, because the personal garden's phone stylesheet sets every link to `color: inherit !important`.
- **Bouquet tile pictures are fitted, not drawn to a box.** `fitThumb` paints with the real renderer into a spare canvas, measures the painted pixels and draws exactly that box at 78 percent of a 44px circle, so all eight flowers and all foliage read at one size. Tiles, chosen stems and the colour panel all use it.
- **Dragging blooms works with a finger.** `bloomAt` takes a slack (16px for touch), and the drag canvas cancels `touchstart` on a bloom and `touchmove` during a drag, since Safari scrolls from a touch that begins on a sticky element despite `touch-action: none`. On a drag canvas the tooltip is hover only.

## Three phone faults with one shape: something invisible eating the tap

**A BUTTON THAT NEEDS PRESSING TWICE IS ALMOST ALWAYS `:hover`.** Safari on iOS has no pointer to hover with, so it fakes one: the first tap on anything carrying a `:hover` rule APPLIES that rule instead of activating the thing, and the tap that does the work is the second. Every lift and colour change in this project was a bare `:hover`, so every one of them could eat a tap. All four stylesheets now turn those effects off under `@media (hover: none)`, which costs nothing, since a lift means nothing on a screen with no cursor to lift away from.

**The other half is the ZOOM DELAY.** A browser waits about 300ms after a tap on anything it might have to double tap to zoom, and a press that does nothing for a third of a second reads as a press that did not register. `touch-action: manipulation` on `button, a, [role=button], summary, input[type=range], label[for]` in all four stylesheets says these are never zoom targets and removes the wait.

**`hidden` LOSES TO ANY AUTHOR RULE, and that is what made the bouquet's foot bar unhideable.** `[hidden]` works through the browser's own stylesheet, which every author rule outranks, so `.bq-sticky-bar { display: flex }` in the phone block kept the bar on screen however many times the code set `bar.hidden = true`. Reading `bar.hidden` back said `true` while the thing was plainly visible, which is the worst kind of disagreement to debug: the JS and the screen each looked right on their own. It needs `.bq-sticky-bar[hidden] { display: none !important }`, in the same block, because that is the rule it is fighting. **Anything in this project that toggles `hidden` on an element whose display is set by a rule needs the same line.**

**THE SCROLL JITTER WAS THE PAGE DUST.** Six sticky canvases re-rasterising thirty times a second on top of a scroll asks the phone to repaint the layer underneath while it is already compositing the scroll. They now skip painting entirely while `html[data-gg-scrolling="1"]`, which is the flag `garden-cursor.js` already sets, so there is one answer to "is the page moving" rather than two. The motes drift a few pixels a second, so half a second of stillness under a thumb is invisible.

**A CARD THAT SEEMS TO OPEN UPWARD is the column moving, not the card.** Opening one closes the others, and if a card higher up was the open one, several hundred pixels vanish ABOVE your thumb and the whole page slides. Nothing animates upward. Scrolling the opened card's own top back to the top of the screen is what makes it read as opening downward from where you pressed.

## Known breakage, do not "fix" by accident, and don't be surprised by it

- (Resolved) `updateResponsiveFlowerLayout()` ended with an `if (width < 720)` block referencing `f` outside the `for...of` that declared it, so it threw a `ReferenceError` on every resize under 720px **and took the rest of `windowResized()` with it**. That stayed harmless only while nothing ran after it; `fitTitle()` does. The cap now runs over `flowers`, which is what the loops above it do, and it was never applied to anything before.
- (Resolved) **The SHARED sketch carried the identical bug and kept it for longer.** Same block, same stray `f`, and there the two calls immediately after it, `rebalanceRowsForNarrowScreens()` and `spreadFlowersHorizontallyForNarrowScreens()`, are exactly the narrow screen layout, so on a phone neither ever ran. It hid for as long as it did because that page had no viewport meta, so a phone reported 980 and the branch was never entered at all; adding the meta made it throw on the first load. **Fixing one sketch is not fixing the other. The two carry hand-ported copies of the same functions, so a bug found in one is a thing to go and look for in the other.**
- `drawFlowersStemsOnly`, `drawFlowersGrowingOnly`, `drawFlowersBloomsOnly` are each **defined twice** in the personal sketch (~lines 1238 and 1284); only the second definition is live. Edit the later one.
- (Resolved) Both garden `index.html` files ran `document.querySelector("[name=viewport]").setAttribute(...)` on iOS against a `<meta>` that was not on the page, so it threw on iPhone and iPad. Both pages now carry a real viewport meta and the script is gone. See **The viewport meta** below, which is the far larger half of this.
- Both garden sketches `loadImage("LOGO-01.png")` relative to their own directory; the file only exists at the repo root, so the in-garden logo 404s.
- `styles.css` `@font-face`s `Mauritius W01 Condensed.ttf` / `Mauritius W01 Italic.ttf`, neither of which is in the repo, so the landing page falls back to Georgia.
- (Resolved) **The daily prompts never came back the next day, and the cause was not the gate.** `setup()` ended with `if (flowers.length === 0) showStep("prompt1"); else showStep("garden")`. That runs after `buildUI()`, so it overrode the gate `buildUI` had just applied: anyone who had ever planted a flower was sent straight to the garden, for good, whatever the date. Both now ask the same question, `showStep(hasPlantedToday() ? "garden" : "prompt1")`.
- (Resolved) **`buildUI()` used to set `step` and toggle the wraps by hand instead of calling `showStep`**, so the daily note was never rendered on load: a returning visitor landed in the garden with no prompts and nothing on screen saying why. It goes through `showStep` now, which also gets the Save button, the tips card and the name field right.
- **`updateScreen()` (`:1594`) is dead. Nothing calls it.** It is `showStep`'s logic again with different reset behaviour, and it never rendered the daily note, which is the bug `buildUI` used to carry. Route through `showStep` rather than reviving it.
- **The marker is a cache; the garden is the truth.** Every flower carries the date it was planted, so `hasPlantedToday()` reads `lastPlanted` first, then requires a flower dated today to corroborate it, and clears the marker when the two disagree. This only ever unblocks. Before it, a marker that said today with no flower behind it shut the prompts off with no way back except clearing storage by hand. `loadGarden()` runs in `preload()`, which p5 finishes before `setup()`, so `flowers` is populated by the time anything asks. To get the prompt flow back for testing, delete today's flower or clear `gratitude_last_planted` (or the account's `last_planted`).
- `*-p5.dom.min.js` is a 0-byte file in both garden folders, still `<script src>`-ed. p5 v1 bundles DOM, so it is harmless.

## Type

**The font is Fraunces, and it is IN the repo.** `Fraunces.ttf` and `Fraunces-Italic.ttf` sit at the root with `Fraunces-OFL.txt` beside them. It replaced Mauritius, whose two files were named by `@font-face` for the project's whole life and never actually existed, so every page had always been rendering Georgia. **If a heading ever looks wrong, check the font is loading before changing a number.**

It is **one variable family** with a weight axis of 100 to 900. The italic is a second face on the same family, so `font-style: italic` picks it up on its own and the bouquet's letter needs nothing else.

**All four pages share it. What differs is the WEIGHT.** The landing page's headings are 700; the two gardens and the bouquet are **600, a real semibold**. That weight was wanted from the start and was impossible under Georgia, which renders 600 identically to 700; measured on Fraunces the same string runs 472.49, 489.11 and 505.73px at 400, 600 and 700, so 600 genuinely sits between. **Buttons are 700 everywhere and are a separate rule.**

**`font-variation-settings: 'SOFT' 50, 'WONK' 0` is on the heading rules, not globally.** SOFT rounds the terminals, which is the friendliness the font was picked for. WONK stays 0 because its slanted alternates are charming in a wordmark and distracting in a paragraph. Body text is Arial and is untouched. The personal sketch sets the same axes inline on its name field and suffix, since inline wins there.

Inline wins over the stylesheet, so the personal sketch's four card headings, its name field and its suffix all set `font-weight: normal` in the JS, and `fitTitle`'s measuring span has to carry the same weight or the width it computes is wrong.

**Georgia has exactly TWO weights, which is why the ratios below exist.** While Georgia was the fallback, 300/400/500 all rendered at 602.72px and 600/700/800 all at 701.91px, so a semibold was impossible and the titles had to earn presence from their RATIO to the line under them. Fraunces fixed the weight problem, but the ratios were right on their own merits and stayed.

| | title | sub | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| landing hero | up to 68 | 17.85 | about 3.4 |
| gardens `.gg-title` / `.gg-sub` | 42 | 16 | 2.63 |
| bouquet `.bq-title` / `.bq-sub` | 28 | 13 | 2.15 |
| the garden's own name and its note | 28 | 13 | 2.15 |

They were 1.73 and 2.22 and read flat next to a 3.4 hero. `fitTitle` derives the note from the title at 0.46, so that ratio holds at every shrunk size. **Check for an inline size before changing `.gg-sub`:** the personal garden's prompt cards set theirs in the JS, so the stylesheet value governs the shared garden's landing and little else there. **Primary buttons are weight 700 everywhere.** The gardens' `.gg-btn` was weight 400 and the personal garden's inline buttons were 600, which made a garden's main action the softest button in the project next to the landing page's bold ones. The two garden pages used `"Inter", sans-serif` throughout and the bouquet used plain `Georgia`, so the four pages read as four websites.

**The bouquet page sets `html { font-size: 17px }`,** the same root the landing page sets, because every size on that page is in rem and on the browser default of 16px they all came out about 6 percent smaller than the same text next door.

**A bouquet step heading is 26px and its line under it 15px.** The sub is not `.gg-sub`'s 18px: that size belongs under a 40px title, and under a 26px one it was nearly as large as the title itself. The personal garden runs a 13px line under a 23px heading, a ratio of 0.57, and 15px under 26px is 0.58.

**Why 26 and not 40.** The gardens carry four heading sizes and it matters which one a thing matches. `.gg-title` at 40px is a title standing on its own above everything, used for Daily Reflection and Choose Your Flower. A heading *inside* a garden card is 26px, the personal garden's Your Flower `h2`, or 23px for its prompt `h3`s, and the shared garden's confirm title is 28px. `.bq-title` sits at the top of a card on all seven steps, so it is the 26px one; at 40px it was the gardens' loudest size used in their quietest position. `.bq-sub` is 18px, which is `.gg-sub` exactly. **Do not widen the flower step to give a heading room:** at 900px its `auto-fit` grid holds four columns and the eight species fall 4 and 4, and past about 960px it becomes five and they fall 5 and 3.

The `@font-face` rules are repeated in all four stylesheets, with `../` paths in the three sub-pages, so every page loads the same family from the same two files at the repo root.

**The personal garden's own title is 26px serif, and it has to fit.** It was 15px Arial against a 21px serif "Garden Tips" in the corner, so the loudest heading on the page was a side note. 26px is the size a heading inside a card takes across this project, and the serif every other page puts its headings in; the note under it is 15px, the 0.58 ratio the bouquet's steps use. `#name-wrap` is `nowrap` and centred so it cannot fall back to a second line, and the field takes 30 characters, so **`fitTitle()` measures the rendered width and shrinks the whole title until it fits**, down to a 13px floor, rather than picking a size from a breakpoint. It runs on load, on every keystroke in the name and on resize, and it returns early under a 200px viewport, since a hidden tab reports 0 and would otherwise shrink the title to the floor and leave it there. The name field carries no right padding: with 4px there the box held the name away from the `'s` and the title read as "Keni 's Gratitude Garden".

Headings on the garden pages come from two places. `.gg-title` and `#tips-card h3` are in the stylesheets; the smaller card headings are built by `buildUI()` with p5.dom and carry inline `font-size` and `font-weight`, so those change in the JS. Inline wins over the stylesheet, so setting a heading's font in CSS alone will not take.

## The uncomfyworld pass: a hero of objects, and a gardener to build

**`https://uncomfyworld.com/` is the reference, and what was taken is its MECHANIC rather than its palette.** Its hero is a scatter of illustrated objects on a dark brown wood desk: each one sparkles, each names itself under the cursor, each goes somewhere. Its Build A Friend is a six page book with a numbered strip along the top, pickers on the left leaf and the thing you are making on the right. **The brown desk did NOT come across.** This project opens on a pale teal sky over a meadow, and a wood ground would have made the hero stop being a garden; the two palettes are opposites and the ground is the one part of that reference this page cannot borrow.

**The other binding constraint is that this repo ships THREE images in total**, `LOGO-01.png`, `apple-touch-icon.png` and `favicon.png`, and draws everything else in code. uncomfyworld's charm is hand painted raster illustration, which is exactly the thing that cannot be copied here. Everything below is inline SVG or canvas in the page's own tokens.

### The hero objects, behind `?hero=2`

**THREE DRAFTS ON ONE GATE, `?hero=2`, `?hero=3` and `?hero=4`**, which a small script at the top of `<body>` reads into `data-hero` on `<html>`. Promoting one is deleting that gate; dropping both is deleting the block. The three places become three objects standing in the meadow: a pot with one bloom, a patch with three, and a wrapped bouquet.

- **`?hero=2` keeps the copy in the MIDDLE with the objects AROUND it.** An arc along the bottom was the first layout and it read as a shelf: three things queued up with a screen of empty sky over them. The reference scatters its objects around something in the middle, and here the thing in the middle is the sentence. Two flank it and one stands below, which is a triangle rather than a row.
- **`?hero=3` is the same scene with no headline at all**, the wordmark being the only type on the page, which is the reference exactly.
- **`?hero=4` IS THE SAME LAYOUT WITH NO GARDEN BEHIND IT.** The meadow is eleven flowers, three hills, a ground, butterflies and motes, and every one of them is a detailed thing competing with the three detailed things in front of it. With it gone the hero is the sky gradient alone, which already ends in cream at the foot, so the objects stand on a pale surface with their own soft shadows under them. That is the reference's arrangement exactly: objects lying on an empty ground and nothing else in the picture.
  - **THE COPY NEEDS ITS SECOND AUTO MARGIN BACK when the meadow goes.** `.hero` is a flex column and `.hero-text-block` is centred by having `margin-top: auto` against `.meadow-scene`'s own `margin-top: auto` below it, the two splitting the free space between them. Hide the meadow and that single auto margin pushes the copy to the BOTTOM of the hero instead, which is the same arithmetic that centres it in the first place read backwards.
- **The shared rules key off a bare `[data-hero]`, not a list of values.** The gate only sets the attribute when `?hero=` is present at all, so the bare selector cannot be matched by accident, and it stops the list growing every time a draft is added. Only the differences between variants name a value.
- **A SMALL WORDMARK top left**, `.hero-mark`, which is what uncomfyworld puts in that corner. It is the only words on `?hero=3` and simply names the place on `?hero=2`.
- **TWO CLOUDS, NOT FIVE, on both.** The five were searched so the sky is never empty for more than 8 seconds, which is right for a hero that is mostly sky; with three objects, a sun, birds, motes and a meadow in it the same five are three too many. The two kept are one from each speed group, so they still overtake each other rather than reading as printed on a sheet.
- **A FIXED COPY WIDTH WAS A BUG, AND IT CAUSED BOTH COMPLAINTS AT ONCE.** It was capped at 540 while the headline's size is a `clamp` on `vw`, so the WIDER the window the more lines the title wrapped to: measured, two lines at 1024 and **four** at about 1400, which is what "crowded in the middle" was. The same constant left lanes of about 430px either side holding a 122px object, which is what "looks empty" was. One cause, two symptoms.
- **`--copy-w` AND `--lane` ARE THE ONLY TWO NUMBERS.** The copy is `min(640px, 46vw)`, the lane is what is left beside it, and the objects' size and position are both derived from the lane, so the two can no longer disagree at any width. The title's clamp came down to `clamp(1.95rem, 3.5vw, 3.05rem)` with it, so it sits on TWO lines at every width.
- **Each flanking object is CENTRED IN ITS OWN LANE**, `left: calc(var(--lane) / 2)`, so it can never overlap the copy however the window changes and it moves outward exactly as fast as the copy's edge does.
- **THE RIGHT HAND PAIR ARE ANCHORED BY `right`, not by `left: 100vw - ...`.** `100vw` counts the scrollbar and `innerWidth` does not, so a right edge computed from it lands about 15px past the window. Anchoring each side to its own edge cancels that.
- **FOUR OBJECTS, TWO TO A LANE, and the fourth is a PERSON.** Build your gardener joins the three places: it is what stops the scatter reading as three variations on a flower, it fills the lower half that one-per-lane left empty, and until it existed the avatar page had no front door anywhere on the site. The two gardens read down the left and the two things you make read down the right.
- **THE SUN MOVES TO THE TOP CENTRE, and it has to.** It lives at `right: 10%`, which is the middle of the right hand LANE, so the object standing there had the sun's rays glowing through it: measured, the bouquet spanned 799 to 972 against a sun at 812 to 944. The corner is not available once the lanes are in use, and the top centre was empty anyway.
- **A PHONE HAS NO LANES, so the copy goes to the TOP and the four objects fill below it in a two by two.** The laptop layout centres the copy with auto margins top and bottom, and on a phone that dropped it straight through the upper pair: measured, the title ran to y 560 with the pot and the bouquet starting at 452, and both labels landed on the subtitle. The sun goes back to the corner there, since with no lanes the corner is free again and a sun in the middle of a phone's sky sits behind the headline.
- **The row fallback is at 1000, not 900.** The lanes stop working the moment one falls under about 210px, which is a window of roughly 1000 against a 640 copy.

- **`z-index: 4` IS THE ONLY VALUE THAT WORKS.** The clouds and the sun are at 2, the birds at 3 and the copy at 5, and the meadow canvas is in normal flow with no z-index of its own. So 4 puts the objects in front of the meadow and behind the headline, which is what standing IN the garden means here.
- **The layer is `pointer-events: none` and each object takes its events back.** A full width sheet over the hero would swallow every tap on the meadow, and a tap on a flower there throws the gardens' own sparkle burst.
- **THE BOB AND THE HOVER LIFT ARE ON TWO DIFFERENT ELEMENTS.** Both are transforms, so on one element the hover simply replaces the idle float and the object DROPS to the ground as you reach for it. The bob is on `.ho-art` and the lift on the `svg` inside it.
- **The shadow is on the anchor, not on the art**, because the art is what floats: a shadow rising and falling with the object reads as the ground moving rather than as the object leaving it.
- **Each object bobs on its own period**, 5.2, 6.4 and 5.8 seconds with negative delays, or the three rise and fall together and the row reads as the page tilting. Same rule the hero's flowers sway by.
- **THE SPARKLE IS `drawNewestSparkles`' OWN GEOMETRY**, four outer points with the inner corners at 0.35 of the radius, which is the shape the gardens throw when a flower is planted and the shape the page's drifting motes are drawn as. Borrowing the reference's would have put a fourth kind of sparkle on a site that already has one.

**THREE OF THE FOUR ARE CANVASES PAINTED BY THE PROJECT'S OWN CODE, not drawings of it.** The bouquet is `renderBouquetCanvas` and every bloom is `drawSpeciesBloom`, both out of `bouquet/bouquet-sketch.js`, which is what the bouquet card further down this page already does. So an icon is a real example and cannot drift from the product. Only the gardener is still inline SVG, and that one is being worked on separately.

- **`drawSpeciesBloom` and `renderBouquetCanvas` are function DECLARATIONS**, so they reach this page's own scripts; `state` and `SPECIES` are `const` and do NOT land on `window`, but a classic script shares the global lexical scope, which is why `state.foliages = ...` resolves at all. The same trap the shared garden hit with `roomKey` and `speciesList`, read the other way round.
- **`.ho-lift` is a THIRD element and it had to be.** `.ho-art` carries the idle bob and the hover is also a transform, and the sparkles are now an svg laid OVER a canvas, so the two have to be lifted together rather than each carrying its own.
- **Each sparkle layer keeps its own viewBox**, matching its canvas's logical box, so a star is never stretched by being laid over a box of a different aspect.
- **THE PERSONAL GARDEN IS A JOURNAL WITH A BLOOM ON IT.** A pot said "a plant"; that page is a flower AND the entry it came from. The pages are drawn before the cover so a sliver of paper shows along the foot, which is what says book rather than card, and the elastic is what says journal rather than book.
- **A PEONY, NOT A ROSE, on the cover.** The 2D rose is concentric circles in one hue, which at icon size is a bullseye rather than a flower. The peony has ten outer petals and a pale centre and still reads as a bloom, and it is not one of the three on the shared patch.
- **THE SHARED PATCH IS THREE DIFFERENT SHAPES, not one shape in three colours**, which is what it was. A daisy is a flat ring of narrow petals, a tulip a closed cup and lavender a spike: the three most unlike each other in the set, and still three with the colour taken out.
- **They are sized against EACH OTHER, not given one radius.** As a multiple of R the set runs tulip 0.61 wide, daisy 0.98, and lavender 0.39 wide by 1.06 tall, so one radius draws a big daisy beside a narrow tulip and a lavender nobody can see. **LAVENDER GROWS UPWARD OUT OF ITS ORIGIN**, so its origin sits at the base of the spike where the others sit at the middle of the bloom.
- **The mound is drawn AFTER the stems so it buries their bases**, which is the order the gardens draw their hills in and for the same reason.
- **THE BOUQUET IS SCALED BY ITS PAINTED HEIGHT, not its width.** It is drawn in a 400 by 500 box and fills about 283 by 391 of it, so it has to be measured rather than sized by its canvas; and because it is taller than it is wide, matching the icon's WIDTH made it about twice the height of everything beside it and it dominated the corner. The drawn icons are 170 units tall in a 200 wide box, so their rendered height is 0.85 of the icon width, and the bouquet is given 1.08 of that. The empty margin is then pulled off the layout box with negative margins, so the label sits against the bouquet rather than against the box.
- **The painter repaints on WIDTH changes only.** iOS collapses its address bar as you scroll, which fires a resize, and rebuilding a bouquet arrangement for a scroll is real work for a picture that has not changed.
- **THE WRAP READ AS A SECOND PLANT POT** until its top edge was gathered. Drawn as a plain trapezoid with a flat rim it was a yellow pot standing beside the terracotta one, which is the same object twice. What makes paper paper is the TOP EDGE: it rises to a corner on each side, dips in the middle where the sheet folds down, and one crease runs the length of it.
- **THE LABEL COLOUR WAS MEASURED, and "it looks pale" was a contrast failure rather than an opacity bug.** At `--darkteal` it is **3.40** against the meadow's `--grass`, under the 4.5 body text needs at 17.3px. It is `--pale-card-ink` at **5.01** there and 6.9 on the sky, so it passes wherever the object happens to stand.
- **THE MIDDLE OBJECT STANDS NEAREST**, lowest and largest, with the outer two set back. Written the other way round it failed twice: on a horizon higher up the screen means further away, so a raised middle read as the far object being the big one, and its top blooms ran into the last line of the subtitle.
- **They OVERLAP the meadow rather than hovering above it.** At the first positions there was a band of empty sky between the objects and the grass and they read as floating in front of the garden.
- **`@media (hover: none)` shows every label and every sparkle.** The reference gets away with hiding its names because a book and an envelope say what they are; a pot and a patch of ground do not say which garden they open.
- **No `cursor` rule is written for these.** `a` is already in `garden-cursor.js`'s clickable list so they take the sparkle pointer for free, and a rule here would lose to it anyway, since every rule that file writes carries `!important`.
- **The hero still carries no button**, and that is not broken by this. The rule was that a button which only scrolls further down the same page is doing what a scroll and the side nav already do; these go to another page.

### The cursor trail

**`garden-cursor.js` grew a third thing, opt in with `data-trail` on its own script tag**, the way `garden-music.js` is asked for a mood on its. `document.currentScript` is captured at the top of the file, since it is only valid while that file is being parsed.

- **IT IS AN ADDITION, NOT A REPLACEMENT.** The two cursor shapes stay exactly as they are, because they do a job the trail cannot: the flower at rest and the sparkle over a control are the site's only signal for what can be pressed, and that took several rounds to settle.
- **THE SPARKLE IS THE PROJECT'S OWN**, four outer points with the inner corners at 0.35 of the radius, which is `drawNewestSparkles` exactly. A trail in any other shape would be a fifth kind of sparkle on a site that has one.
- **ONLY `index.html` ASKS FOR IT.** The gardens already carry butterflies, birds, motes and a planting burst, and a trail there is one moving thing too many.
- **A DARK LINE INSIDE A WARM FILL**, which is the argument the cursor itself is built on: no single colour works on every ground this page has. On cream the gold body measures about 1.1 against the paper and the dark edge is what draws it; on the dark teal band the edge vanishes and the gold body carries it.
- **A STILL POINTER SPAWNS NOTHING.** Without the 15px distance gate a pointer resting on a control piles a sparkle on the same pixel every frame and the trail becomes a bright blob sitting under the hand.
- **THE LOOP RUNS ONLY WHILE SOMETHING IS ALIVE**, and stops when the last sparkle dies. A permanent `requestAnimationFrame` on a page that already paints six sticky canvases is a cost for nothing.
- **`z-index: 99999` and `pointer-events: none`.** A trail that stopped at a modal would read as the pointer going behind it, and with no pointer events there is nothing it can ever intercept.
- **Off entirely under `(hover: none)` and `(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)`**, and it ignores `pointerType === "touch"`: a touch screen has no pointer to trail.

### `avatar/`, build your gardener

**THE GARDENER STANDS IN BOTH GARDENS NOW, which is the promise the build page's first screen has always made and nothing kept.**

- **They paint just before `drawForegroundHill()` in BOTH sketches**, so the hill buries their feet exactly the way it buries a stem. In the personal garden that is after the front row, so they stand in front of the flowers, and `drawAllDates()` runs later still, so a gardener can never hide a date label. **The two sketches order their own hill differently** (the shared one draws front BLOOMS after its hill), so the insertion point is named by the hill, not by a line number.
- **The feet follow the ground, not a guess at it.** `gardenerHillTop` and `sharedHillTop` each re-use the exact expression their own `drawForegroundHill` draws its edge with, and the two differ: the personal hill is `height * 0.75` less a bump of `height * 0.02` at `noise(x * 0.002)`, the shared one `height * 0.76` less a FIXED 20 at `noise(x * 0.004)`.
- **Sunk by a tenth of their own height**, so what the hill covers is the shoes and a little ankle at any window size. A fixed pixel amount buries a phone gardener to the knee.
- **SIZED AGAINST THE SMALLER OF THE TWO DIMENSIONS**, `min(height * 0.19, width * 0.34)` clamped to 96 and 180. Taken off the height alone a phone drew somebody 154 tall in a window 375 wide, which is a person filling the garden they are standing in.
- **Personal garden: BOTTOM CENTRE**, because it is the only quadrant with nothing in it. The Garden Tips card owns bottom left, the friends button bottom right, and the daily note and the four round icons own the top.
- **Shared garden: ONLY YOURS, and only beside your own flower.** Twenty gardeners in one meadow is a lobby rather than a garden, which is the conclusion the 3D exploration already reached: what is left of somebody who was here for a minute is their flower, not a figure of them. **No flower, no gardener**, since the build page says they stand beside the one you planted that day. They stand on whichever side of it has room, since always standing to the left puts a gardener half off a 375 wide screen whenever a flower lands near the edge; measured at five flower positions across the width, every one lands fully on screen with 15px buried.
- **The three tags go in the HEAD of both garden pages, not at the foot of the body.** The sketch is INJECTED once the session check resolves and that is a network race: on a warm cache it can resolve before the body has finished parsing. `bouquet/bouquet-sketch.js` is among them because the flower they wear is the REAL 2D bloom; it costs **227KB** against the 898KB of p5 and 231KB of p5.party already there, and the alternative is an eighth hand-ported copy of the flower maths.

**A GARDENER TAKES A HEAD PAT: one eye winks and a heart rises out of the crown.** The two halves are deliberately in different files, because they are different questions. **`AvatarDraw.patFx(elapsed)` decides what a pat LOOKS like** and `GardenGardener.pat()` / `patNow()` decide **when one is running**, so the studio's figure and the gardener standing in either garden cannot drift apart on either half.

- **THE WINK IS AN ARC, NOT A DASH.** A flat line reads as a closed eye on a sleeping face; a shallow curve bending UP is what a wink is, and it is the mouth's own stroke weight so the two belong to one drawing. The smile widens a little with it, or the face reads as having something in its eye rather than as pleased.
- **THE HEART LEAVES THE TOP OF THE HEAD, not the middle of the face**, since the flower is on the head and anything leaving from there would be leaving the bloom. It rises and drifts out, which is the drift the planting sparkle already has, and fades over its last 40 percent.
- **The effects are MODULE STATE for the length of one paint**, set by `paint`/`paintAt`'s optional fourth argument and cleared on the way out. Threading them through four signatures is a lot of surface for one optional flag that only the face and the heart ever read.
- **THE GARDENER IS ASKED BEFORE THE FLOWERS**, in both gardens' pointer handlers. They are painted in front of the front row, so a bloom behind them must not answer a tap that landed on a person.
- **The hit box is the BODY, not the 360 box.** Most of that box is clear margin above the flower, and a tap in empty sky two heads up should not pat anybody.
- **ONLY THE STUDIO NEEDS A LOOP.** Both gardens already redraw sixty times a second, so there `patNow()` is simply read on the next frame. The build page is still, so a pat starts a `requestAnimationFrame` that stops itself the moment the pat is over; a permanent loop on a still page is a cost for nothing, which is the rule the cursor trail already follows.
- **A second pat while one is running RESTARTS the clock** rather than stacking a second loop: somebody patting repeatedly should get a wink each time, not a queue of them.
- **The landing band's five do NOT take a pat**, and that is the links deciding. They carry `?t=` and navigate, so a 1100ms pat would never finish; they bob instead.

**A FRIEND'S GARDENER IS THEIR AVATAR IN THE FRIENDS PANEL, and `avatarFor` is the one place that decides.** Their gardener if they have built one, **their initial if they have not, and NEVER a default figure in place of a missing one**, or every friend who has not been near the build page appears to have made the same gardener. It is guarded on `AvatarDraw` and on `drawSpeciesBloom` as well as on the look, because this module loads on pages that may not carry the drawing at all.

- **`GardenGardener.bubble(look, px)` draws it**, the same figure the gardens stand, at 1.16 of the circle so the flower does not clip: the figure's box carries clear margin at the top, and at 1.00 it floated in the middle with a ring of background round it. `.ga-av[data-gardener]` puts it on the garden's own pale sky rather than the initial's teal disc, which the figure's own teal merged into, and `overflow: hidden` makes the circle a crop rather than a frame.
- **`showMyGardener` is the door for somebody with an account, AND ONLY THEM.** Signed out, that button opens the sign in card rather than this panel, so nothing there is ever reached; the landing page's gardener band is the door for everybody else. It reads the look through `GardenGardener.get()` rather than off `profile`, so the panel never has to know whether it came from the account or the browser.
- **`GardenGardener.href()` works out where the build page is from its OWN script tag**, captured as `document.currentScript` at parse time, so a link to it is correct from the root, from a garden one level down, or from anywhere added later. `garden-cursor.js` reads its own tag the same way for `data-trail`.

**A gardener is KEPT now, and `garden-gardener.js` at the repo root is the one place a look is read and written.** One copy, like `garden-music.js`, `garden-life.js`, `garden-journal.js`, `garden-cursor.js` and `garden-account.js`; four pages need to know who your gardener is and a second copy of the rule would drift the way the flower maths already has in seven places.

- **IT OWNS THE LOOK, NOT THE DRAWING.** `avatar-draw.js` draws and this decides where the row of numbers lives. The split is worth it because the drawing is 1100 lines that only the picker and the gardens need and this is forty that everything needs.
- **The account if there is one, this browser if there is not, NEVER BOTH**, which is `GardenStore`'s rule exactly, debounced the same 400ms. Writing to both sounds safer and is not: a stale browser copy would then shadow a newer account one the next time somebody signed in on an old laptop. The local key is `gg_gardener`.
- **IT IS THE `profiles` ROW, NOT THE GARDEN ROW, and the shared garden is the reason.** That page has no garden row for anybody, since planting there needs no account. It does have profiles, and `garden-account.js` already selects them to build the friends list, so a friend's gardener arrives with the name it is drawn beside instead of costing a query each. **Step 9 of `SUPABASE-SETUP.md`** adds the column; there is no new policy because `auth.uid() = id` on update already covers it.
- **THE TWO FRIENDS QUERIES WENT TO `select("*")`, AND THAT IS A GUARD.** Supabase answers a query naming a column that does not exist with a 400 and an error OBJECT rather than a throw, so on any project where Step 9 has not been run, naming `look` would empty the result and take every friend's NAME down with it. A star selects whatever the table actually has. `loadProfile` has always done this.
- **A missing column and an empty one are different.** `look` undefined means Step 9 has not been run and the browser's copy should stand; `look` null means the account genuinely has no gardener yet, and it should also stand, or signing in would wipe one built here beforehand.
- **`touched` is what stops an account arriving mid-edit.** The session check is a network round trip, so somebody can have picked a flower before it resolves, and a gardener reverting under the hand choosing it would be the worst bug on the page. One capturing listener on `.av-left` sets it, rather than a line in each of the nine builders that would be forgotten in the tenth.
- **`AvatarDraw.paintAt(ctx, look, cx, footY, h)` stands somebody on ground somebody else painted**, and `paint` and it now share one `paintFigure` that neither scales nor clears. **The anchor is the SOLE**, because a figure hung by its top edge sinks or floats as the flower on its head changes height. `FOOT` is 316, the lowest pixel any shoe reaches, and it is a CONSTANT rather than a measured box: measuring means reading back 86,000 pixels and this runs inside a sixty frame draw loop. **`h` is the whole 360 box, not the painted height**, so every species comes out the same person; fitting what is painted to one height would give a tulip gardener, whose flower grows up on a stem, a smaller body than a daisy one.
- **The last page KEEPS the gardener.** It used to reset the whole look, so the only thing the final button did was undo the six pages that led to it. Start again is still there, as a text link at `.av-plain` weight, because it must never compete with Keep.

**IT IS A STUDIO NOW, NOT A SIX PAGE BOOK, and the book went because the gardener is SAVED.** Four files beside the project's other sub-pages: `avatar-index.html`, `avatar-style.css`, `avatar-draw.js` and `avatar-sketch.js`.

- **THREE SCREENS, THE SAME SHAPE AS THE BOUQUET**: a gallery to start from, one studio you live in, and a page that says the gardener is kept. `data-av` on `<html>` names which is showing, the way the bouquet's `CHROMELESS` does, and the page's own 44px title stands down on the studio: a heading and its line over a screen-tall studio is 150px of the one thing a studio needs, which is height.
- **THE BOOK WAS RIGHT UNTIL THE GARDENER COULD BE KEPT.** Six pages is a lovely way to meet something once, and the uncomfyworld reference IS a book; but the second visit is an EDIT, and an edit in a book means turning five pages to reach the shoes. It also leaves the project with ONE customiser pattern rather than two, which is the same argument that put `garden-music.js` at the root instead of in four copies. **The other direction was considered and refused**: the bouquet WAS a seven step wizard and was rebuilt into the studio on purpose, and every reason is written down above.
- **NOTHING WAS REWRITTEN TO DO IT.** `buildStudio` MOVES the controls out of `#avSource` into the panes, exactly as `buildStudio` does in `bouquet-sketch.js`, so every grid keeps its id and its listeners and a control cannot drift from the thing that built it. `#avSource` is emptied and removed at boot.
- **FIVE TABS, WHICH IS THE BOUQUET'S FIVE EXACTLY**: Skin, Hair, Flower, Clothes, Carrying. Skin is its own tab rather than sharing with hair, and that is not symmetry for its own sake: together they were 8 skins, 6 fringes, 6 backs and 12 hair colours, **32 tiles in one panel**, which on a phone is a long scroll before you reach what you came for.
- **THE NAME MOVED OFF ITS OWN SCREEN** and sits beside the figure. One field never needed a page.
- **`hidden` LOSES TO ANY AUTHOR RULE**, so `.av-studio[hidden] { display: none !important }` is load bearing: without it the studio's own `display: grid` keeps it on screen underneath the gallery however many times the code sets `hidden`, and reading the property back reports `true` while the thing is plainly visible. The bouquet's foot bar needed the identical line.
- **WHERE IT OPENS, and the order matters.** A `?t=` from the landing page wins, since somebody pressed that gardener on purpose; otherwise a gardener already kept opens the STUDIO wearing them, because coming back is an edit; only a first visit sees the gallery.
- **THE DONE PAGE CARRIES THE FIGURE.** It said "your gardener" over a card with no gardener on it, which is the one screen where the figure is the whole point.

**FIVE TEMPLATES, AND THEY LIVE IN `avatar-draw.js` RATHER THAN ON EITHER PAGE THAT SHOWS THEM.** The landing page draws them in a row under the three garden cards and the build page opens on the same five as a gallery, so a look written in two files would be two rows that drift; `avatar-draw.js` is the one file both pages already load. They are unlike each other in every slot rather than one gardener in five colours, and **a SPROUT species is among them**, twice over since the hibiscus joined them, because a flower on a stem looks unlike the five that ring a face and a gallery of rings alone would hide half the set. A blank sixth tile, "Build your own", is IN the grid rather than a link under it, which is the bouquet's rule: starting from nothing is one of the six choices rather than the thing you fall back to.

- **A GARDENER IS SOMEBODY, NOT AN OUTFIT: four questions and one free line, on a SIXTH tab.** Favourite thing to do, cosiest place, favourite snack, and the flower you would plant every day, then a line in your own words. It is the only step that changes nothing about the drawing.

- **PICKS RATHER THAN FOUR TEXT BOXES.** Four open fields is a form, every card in the guest book would come out a different length, and it is four things to moderate rather than one. The free line is LAST so the quick part is done first.
- **CHIPS, NOT DROPDOWNS.** A dropdown hides seven of the eight answers behind a press, and this is the one step meant to be quick. Tapping the chosen one clears it, which is the rule every other slot on this page follows.
- **THE FOURTH QUESTION IS THE PROJECT'S OWN.** A hobby, a place and a snack are the reference's three; which flower you would plant every day is the one that could only be asked here, and it is the eight species so the answer means something the rest of the site already knows.
- **IT RIDES INSIDE `look` AS `about`, SO IT NEEDS NO NEW COLUMN AND NO NEW QUERY.** The whole look already saves to `profiles.look`, which `garden-account.js` already selects to draw a friend's gardener, so somebody's answers arrive with the figure they belong to.
- **`tints` AND `about` ARE BOTH COPIED OUT OF `DEFAULT_LOOK`, NEVER SHARED.** `Object.assign` is shallow and `DEFAULT_LOOK` holds one object for each, so without that every gardener built in a session would be writing into the same two objects, and into the template everybody else starts from.
- **`font: inherit` ALONE IS NOT ENOUGH ON A TEXTAREA.** The shorthand is reset by the browser's own stylesheet for form controls, so the family has to be named or the note comes out in monospace beside eight chips that are not.
- Six tabs against the bouquet's five. Checked at 375: the rail scrolls, the last tab ends at 357 of 375, and the page has no sideways scroll.

**A LOOK IS DATA, and that is the whole design rather than an implementation detail.** `{name, skin, hair, hairCol, flower, top, shoulders, held}` is very nearly the shape `garden3d/avatar-kit.js` already builds a 3D avatar from, including its `SKINS` and `HAIRS`. So ONE saved row renders as a flat drawing on the web and as a figure in the 3D exploration. A row of numbers survives being saved against an account and read back next year; a picture does not. **Change one of those lists and change the other.**

**THE FLOWER IS WORN AS A HOOD, AND IT IS NOT A KEEPSAKE.** Four references were given and each answers a different question: a flat vector people set for the BODY, a sticker set of round characters for the FACE (two dots, two cheeks, a mouth, nothing else), a child's face inside a five petalled flower for the HOOD, and two soft 3D characters whose flower GROWS out of the head. Every gardener wears a flower and the species is part of the LOOK, chosen on its own step, which is what makes the figure part of the garden rather than a person standing in it.

- **THE HOOD IS THE SPECIES' OWN 2D DRAWING, SAT BEHIND THE FACE, and that is the third answer to this and the one that holds.** It was `drawSpeciesBloom` at a large radius first, then a set of STYLISED petals described by a count, a half width and a tip number. The stylised version read as **a ring of separate blobs** and it failed three ways at once, all measurable: the petal bases sat at radius 56 against a face of 44, so a bare twelve pixel ring of background showed between the flower and the head; at six to eight petals nothing touched its neighbour, so the gaps were as loud as the petals; and with only a count and a tip to tell them apart, a rose and a poppy were very nearly the same picture.

- **Drawing the real bloom fixes all three at once and cannot drift from the product.** The 2D blooms already overlap heavily (a daisy's sixteen petals span 43 degrees each on a 22.5 degree spacing) and they already hide their own bases, so both faults were the stylised drawing's own and not a property of flowers.
- **`R` per species is chosen so the petal TIPS land near 96 from the middle and everything the species keeps in its CENTRE falls inside the face's own 44.** Read off the 2D functions: daisy petals span 0.12R to 1.00R with a centre disc of 0.20R; sunflower 0.095R to 1.345R with a disc of **0.675R**; poppy tips at 1.00R with a 0.20R eye; peony 1.00R with a 0.135R centre; rose about 1.01R with 0.17R of concentric circles; lily 1.08Rl with a 0.39R throat.
- **THE SUNFLOWER IS THE ONE THAT HAS TO BE COMPROMISED, and it is why R is 70 there rather than 96.** Its disc is as wide as its petals are long, so at a radius that put its tips with the others the disc came out at 48 against a 44 face and ringed it in dark olive, which is the "dark collar" the first attempt was abandoned for. At 70 the disc leaves a **three pixel rim**, which reads as an outline rather than a collar, and the tips land at 94.
- **`dy` is the peony's alone.** Its painted box is deliberately asymmetric, 0.08R high, so it needs pushing down 8px to sit centred on the face. Everything else is 0.
- **`petal()`, the stylised petal routine, is DELETED.** Do not reintroduce it: the ring of blobs is what it produced both times.
- **THE ORCHID IS 70 WITH A `dy` OF 10 AND EVERY OTHER RING SPECIES IS 84, and it is the only one of the eight that needs both numbers.** Its painted box is both lopsided and WIDER THAN TALL, 1.21 R across against 1.10 down, so unlike every other ring species no single radius can land its tips at 84 on all three sides. The sides win, because the bonnet's width is what reads as a hood: sides 84.4, top and bottom about 77. (It was `R: 80, dy: 18` against the earlier 1.04 by 1.06 box, where all three did land within a pixel of 84.) (The retired peony was the earlier case: 92 and a `dy` of 7, for the 0.08 R its outer ring sits above its origin.)
- **RE-TUNE THE SIX `R` VALUES if any species' 2D drawing changes**, the same rule `BLOOM_BOX`, `HALO_BOX` and `BLOOM_METRICS` already carry.

- **THREE OF THE EIGHT GROW INSTEAD OF BEING WORN, and they do NOT all grow for the same reason.** A tulip is a closed cup and lavender is a spike, so neither has a ring of petals to put a face inside. The hibiscus has one and still sprouts: its stamen column reaches 1.13 R, past every petal, and as a hood that column came out from behind the head and lay across the shoulder. Each sprout entry in `HOOD` therefore carries its own `note`, which is the sentence the picker prints, because one line written for all three is wrong about whichever it was not written for. Worn on top they read as **a chef's hat and a spiky crown**; an inverted tulip and a fan of three spikes were both tried and both failed. They now get a plain cowl round the face and the flower on a short stem out of the crown, with two leaves on it, which is what makes it a sprout rather than a flower taped to a head. **Those two ARE `drawSpeciesBloom`**, since on a stem that is exactly what it draws.
- **THE HEAD SITS ON THE BODY, it does not hover over it.** With the body at `y: 188` the face's own bottom edge was 162 and the body began 26px below it, so the background showed between them and the figure read as a head balanced on a separate object. There is no neck at all, which is how every one of the references is built.
- **TWO SMALL HANDS AND NO ARMS.** That is what "arms behind the body" means here and what the sprout characters do: a rounded hand at each side with the limb hidden. It also removes the stick arms, which read as twigs.
- **THE LEGS ARE 25 APART, NOT 19, and that is the shoes' doing rather than the legs'.** At 19 the two shoes were 26 wide on centres 19 apart, so they overlapped by 6 and the feet read as one dark block. The leg spacing is set by whatever has to stand on it.
- **ACCESSORIES, NOT KEEPSAKES.** Nothing is earned, nothing is locked and nothing is tied to a species: the flower on the head already carries the garden, and eight locked tiles were a reward system bolted onto a dressing up screen. Eight accessories across four slots (`neck`, `body`, `feet`, `hold`), **one per slot**, so two choices can never fight over the same place on the figure. Four small rows rather than one grid of eight, because a grid says "pick some of these" where the truth is "pick one of each".
**ONE LAYER RULE: THE FLOWER FIRST, THEN THE BODY, THEN THE HEAD.** A hood is behind whoever is wearing it, so its lower petals go behind the shoulders, and everything worn on the torso is simply on the torso. Three arrangements were tried before this and each failed differently: the flower behind at the old proportions read as a fan propped up; the flower in FRONT made an apron look printed onto the petals; separating them left the two sprout species with their heads floating, and the neck grown to fix that was worse. **A RING FLOWER ALWAYS REACHES BELOW THE CHIN** and no proportion avoids it, since for petals to show around a face of radius 44 the bloom has to be bigger than 44.

**THE HEAD IS BUILT IN THREE RINGS, AND GETTING THIS WRONG BROKE FIVE ROUNDS OF HAIR.**

| | what it is |
|---|---|
| `HEAD` | the centre the bonnet and its opening are struck from |
| `CROWN` | the SOLID shape of the bonnet, 58 by 56, with the petals radiating off it |
| `OPEN` | the FACE OPENING cut in that bonnet, 55 by 53 |
| `FACE` | the skin, 44 by 45, sat LOW in the opening |

- **THE LAST LINE IS THE WHOLE TRICK.** In every hood reference the hair is a solid mass FILLING the opening and the face is a smaller oval sitting low inside it, so what is left over reads as a fringe across the brow and a piece down each cheek **without any of it having to be drawn as hair**. Ours had no opening at all, only a circle of skin on a ring of petals, so hair could only ever be painted onto the face or onto the flower. Five versions were tried that way and the verdict on the last was "boxy", which is exactly what drawing hair-shaped rectangles into a space with no edge of its own produces.
- **THE SOLID CROWN is what makes the flower a bonnet** rather than a ring of petals. It closes the gaps between the petal bases and the petals become the ears and trim on it.
- **THERE IS NO RIM ROUND THE OPENING.** A deeper ring was drawn there for a round, meant to say the face was inside the bonnet, and it read as **a hoop drawn on the head**. The hair's own edge does that job.
- **The crown came down from 66 by 63 to 58 by 56.** At the larger size it covered two thirds of a bloom whose tips reach 84, so the daisy and the sunflower read as bonnets rather than as those species.
- **A LENGTH GROWS OUT OF THE CROWN, it does not stand beside the head.** It was a shape struck at a fixed offset from the middle with a flat top, so however it was tapered it still read as a plank parked at the ear. `lengthPath` starts as a POINT up at the scalp, swings out past the jaw and tapers to a rounded tip, which is the line hair actually falls in. It is drawn in a right-hand frame around the head's own centre and mirrored, so the two sides cannot drift apart. Straight-sided bars with a flat top and a squared end are what **"boxy"** meant, and the braids escaped it only because a stack of discs cannot be square.
- **A PIGTAIL IS GATHERED AT THE TOP**, so its upper section is that same curve run only as far as the tie. Drawn as a rounded rectangle parked at the side it had a flat top and no root.
- **THE FRINGE IS SOFT WITH TWO GENTLE POINTS**, off the fruit-kid reference. Five hard scallops read as a cog.
- **ANYTHING LONGER THAN THE FACE GOES BEHIND THE FLOWER.** `drawHairLong` paints before the bonnet, so a plait or a length is hidden by the crown and the petals and emerges at the bonnet's LOWER CORNERS, which is what the bear, the chick, the penguin and the cat in the reference sheet all do.
- **The order on the head is therefore**: long hair, flower, body, then the mass clipped to the opening, the face oval, the fringe clipped to the face, the ears, the marks.
- **TWO SMALL EARS at the sides of the opening.** Every one of the hood references has them and they are most of why those heads read as heads rather than as discs.
- **THE FRINGE AND THE BACK ARE TWO SEPARATE CHOICES.** They were nine fixed styles, so a blunt fringe could only ever come with a bob and a side parting only ever with one long piece. Split, **twelve tiles buy thirty-six looks where nine used to buy nine**, and the two halves are drawn in different places anyway: a fringe is clipped to the face and a back falls out of the bonnet from the crown. `FRINGES` is Blunt, Parted, Side part, High, Curly, Choppy; `BACKS` is Tucked in, Bob, Long, Wavy, Braids, Buns.
- **A fringe tile shows its fringe on a tucked-in head and a back tile shows its back under a blunt fringe**, so each tile is only ever about the one thing it changes.
- **THE TWO LENGTHS MEET BEHIND**, which is what makes a head of hair look full rather than like two strips hung at the ears. The join paints before the flower and the body, so almost all of it is hidden and what shows is the hair in the gaps between the bonnet's edge and the shoulders, which is exactly where the back of somebody's hair is.
- **BUNS ARE BUNCHES LOW AT THE SIDES, not space buns on top.** A bun above the head is exactly where the flower is, so a true pair would be entirely behind the bonnet and never seen; at 81 from the middle against the petals' 84 they clear its edge, which is where the bear and the cat in the hood references wear theirs. A ponytail still cannot exist at all.
- **THE HAIR LEAVES THE HEAD AT THE HEAD'S WIDEST POINT, and the dip at the ear was ARITHMETIC rather than a badly drawn curve.** The length started at x 8 near the top of the scalp and only reached 52 by the jaw, while the crown is 58 wide from about y 100 to y 140. The silhouette is the MAXIMUM of the two shapes, and **the maximum of a narrowing curve and a widening one has a MINIMUM where they cross**, which is a notch about eight pixels deep at the ear. No amount of tapering the length could have removed it, and several rounds were spent trying. The first bezier in `lengthPath` is now a quarter of the crown's own ellipse, controls at 0.5523 of each radius, so the hair runs out along the head and falls from there.
- **`drawCrown` FILLS THE WHOLE CROWN IN HAIR COLOUR BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE**, so there is no seam where the top of the head stops being head and starts being hair: the lengths grow out of a solid mass rather than being hung either side of one. It paints before the flower, so on the six ring species the bonnet covers all of it and nothing changes; **on the tulip and the lavender, which wear their flower on a stem and have no bonnet, it IS the top of the head.** It runs for every back style including Tucked in.
- **A WAVE MAY ONLY HAPPEN BELOW y 176, which is the bottom of the crown.** Above that the crown is the wider of the two shapes, so an inward swing up there cuts back into the head and puts the notch straight back. **Wavy is therefore 112 where Long is 104**, longer than the style it is meant to differ from: at 98 there were only forty pixels of hair left under the crown to wave in and it came out as a second Long.
- **BLUNT AND WAVY WERE WITHIN A FEW PIXELS OF EACH OTHER**, so one had to become something else. The soft two-point shape kept the slot and the name Blunt; **Choppy**, four uneven points, replaced the other.
- **A CENTRE PARTING IS HIGH IN THE MIDDLE AND LOW AT THE TEMPLES.** Parted was drawn the other way up, so it read as a fringe with a notch cut out of it.
- **A PARTING'S APEX IS NOT ITS JUNCTION, which is why Side part read as sitting off the side of the head.** Its two curves met at `F.x - 2`, which looks central written down, but the second control at `F.x - 22` dragged the actual high point about ten pixels further left again. Both moved right by ten, so the high point is now where the junction always claimed it was. **This is the quadratic control trap for the third time in this project**, after the basket's handle and the wave's swing: the curve passes nowhere near the point that aims it.
- **THE DONE PAGE CARRIES NO ROW OF NUMBERS.** It printed "skin 1, Parted fringe and Wavy in 9, poppy at hue 205, top hue 210" at somebody who had just made a person, which is the inside of the thing rather than the thing. That a look IS a row of numbers is worth saying once, in words, and the quiet note under it already does.
- **THE MASS IN THE OPENING IS SMOOTH FOR EVERY STYLE.** Curly used to scallop it as well as the hairline, which made the whole top of the head lumpy under a bonnet that is already round. Curly is a fringe and nothing else.

**EVERY SLOT IS THREE ITEMS IN THREE COLOURS: nine tiles in three clean rows.** Every colour is its own tile, at three columns so an item's three land side by side: three Boots, then three Clogs, then three Wellies. A swatch row under the chosen item was tried first and it put the colours a step away from the choice and hid them until something was picked.

- **THERE ARE NO `None` TILES AND NO `plain`.** Tapping the item already chosen takes it off. An empty clothes slot IS the plain body, whose colour is the row of six directly above it, so a Plain tile was that same choice printed twice; `NO_NONE` is now empty and kept only so a slot could be made compulsory again.
- **WHAT IS HELD AND WHAT IS ON THE FACE CARRY NO COLOURS**, and they are one row each. Three watering cans in three greens is the same choice printed three times.
- **A Backpack and a Trug basket were built to even the rows out and both were cut. THE BASKET IS BACK, redrawn; the backpack is not.** The backpack read as a **letter H**, since from the front all a backpack is is two straps and a chest clip, and nothing fixes that. The trug was a shallow dark oval and read as a **hammock**, which is what a wide flat curve reads as whatever is drawn round it. What names the new one is DEPTH: a wall taller than the mouth is wide, three woven courses with uprights between them clipped inside it, a rim, and an arched handle whose feet disappear under that rim. The shoulder keeps two items and the hand now has three.
- **A QUADRATIC CURVE PASSES NOWHERE NEAR ITS CONTROL POINT, and the basket's handle was invisible because of it.** Its top sits at a quarter, a half and a quarter of the three points, so a control at `y - 22` drew an arch topping out at `y - 3`, which is inside a fist of radius 13: the handle was drawn, was correct, and could not be seen. The control is at `y - 70` to put the arch at `y - 21`. **This is a general Canvas fact and it will bite again** anywhere a curve is aimed by eye at a height it has to clear.
- **Freckles are SIX dots at radius 1.4, across the nose and the inner cheek only.** They were twelve at 2.1 reaching x plus or minus 28, which on a face of 44 is the whole width of it, and at that size a freckle reads as a spot. They sit BELOW the eye line, which reach `F.y + 11.5`, and CLEAR OF THE MOUTH, which runs from `F.y + 18` across x plus or minus 6, so the band is 13 to 19 and stays outside both. **The pair out at y 25 went in a second pass**: that is level with the corners of the mouth, and a freckle beside a mouth reads as something on the chin.

**THE PALETTE WAS OPENED UP, AND THE COMPLAINT THAT DROVE IT WAS "NO WHITE OR BLACK".** It was true in three separate places at once, which is why it took three separate fixes rather than one list getting longer.

| | was | is |
|---|---|---|
| `TINTS`, every garment and accessory | 6 hues, each item offering a curated **three** | **10**, and every item may take any of them |
| `TOPS`, the plain body | 6 bare hues at a FIXED saturation of 26 | **12** entries carrying their own `s` and `l`, four of them neutral |
| `HAIRS` | 12 | **16** |
| a flower's colour | hue only, sat and light pinned to the species | `flowerSat` and `flowerLight` ride alongside |

- **EVERY ONE OF THOSE LISTS IS APPENDED TO, NEVER REORDERED, and that is now permanent.** `skin`, `hairCol` and every entry in `look.tints` are INDICES, so slotting White in where it belongs tonally would repaint every gardener already saved. The four new tints and the four new hairs are on the END, out of tonal order, and that is correct rather than sloppy. `garden3d/avatar-kit.js` carries `SKINS` and `HAIRS` and moved with them.
- **`tintsFor(id)` RETURNS THE ITEM'S OWN THREE FIRST and then everything else.** The old note said six denims of a dungaree is a paint chart and three is a choice; that was right about the ROW and wrong about the WARDROBE. Ordering keeps the starting point without refusing anything. `suggestedFor(id)` is the old three, for anything that wants only them.
- **THE PICKER HAD TO BE RESTRUCTURED, because ten colours cannot be one tile each.** It was one tile per item PER COLOUR, three Boots then three Clogs then three Wellies, which at ten would have been **thirty tiles in the feet slot alone**. It is now one tile per item with a wrapping colour row underneath. A swatch row was tried once before and rejected for putting the colours a step away from the choice and hiding them until something was picked; both halves are answered by the row being ALWAYS on screen and saying "Pick one above to colour it" when the slot is empty. Measured at 375: ten swatches on two rows, furthest edge 348, no sideways scroll.
- **A TILE THAT IS NOT WORN SHOWS THE COLOUR IT WOULD ARRIVE IN**, not the slot's current tint. Without that line every tile in the row repainted itself in the worn garment's colour, which is the exact fault the per-tile tint was added to fix in the first place.
- **WHITE IS LIGHTNESS 93 AND BLACK IS 15, NOT 100 AND 0.** `tone` shades a garment by moving its lightness and clamps at 8 and 96, so at a true white every fold, hem and collar clamps to the same value and the garment comes out as one flat silhouette.
- **`look.top` STILL STORES A HUE**, because every gardener saved so far holds one. The saturation and lightness ride as OPTIONAL `topS` and `topL` defaulting to the old 26 and 56, so an old look reads back as exactly the colour it was. `bodyCol(L, dl, ds)` is the one place that resolves them; it replaced `hsl(L.top, 26, 56)` written out at **five** call sites, which is what made a neutral body impossible to add at all.
- **`TOPS` IS TWELVE FOR THE GRID, NOT THE PALETTE.** That row is three columns, so ten falls 3, 3, 3 and 1 and ends on an orphan tile.
- **A FLOWER COULD NOT BE WHITE, and no hue reaches it**: white is an absence of saturation. `flowerSat` is a second slider on the Flower tab, and **the lightness rides with it** rather than being a third control, because a bloom drained at the species' own lightness comes out GREY, which is not what anybody reaching for pale means. Picking a different species resets all three, or a daisy drained to white would hand its white to the poppy chosen after it.

**THE FIVE TEMPLATES ARE PEOPLE NOW, AND "BUILD YOUR OWN" IS FIRST.** Nares, Alice, Mary, Bean and Juno, drawn from references rather than assembled to be unlike each other. Build your own moved from the blank sixth tile to the first: last, it reads as what you fall back to when none of the five fit; first, it is simply the first of the six ways to start, which is what it is.

- **BEAN AND JUNO ARE NODS, NOT COPIES, AND THEY ARE NAMED FOR THEMSELVES.** Anybody who knows the beagle and the boy will see them; nothing on the page claims to be a character that is not this project's, which matters on a public site attached to a degree.
- **THEY ARE ALSO WHY THE PALETTE HAD TO OPEN.** Between them they need a white body, a black crop, a white hood, a black boot and a navy short, and before this round the figure could make **none** of those.
- **`startFrom` WAS WIPING EVERY TEMPLATE'S COLOURS.** `{ tints: {} }` came last in its `Object.assign`, so a template naming a black boot or a cream apron had all of it thrown away the instant it was chosen. It copies them now, and copies rather than shares the object, or editing a gardener would write into the template everybody else starts from.
- **BEAN'S BODY SITS A STEP UNDER THE HOOD**, 86 against 94. At the same white the ruff, the face and the torso measured within five values of each other and the whole figure came out as one white blob with a pair of boots under it.
- Every template was checked by RENDERING and reading the painted pixels back, not by eye: Mary's peony first came out `#bdab9e`, a greyed taupe rather than the reference's tan, and is `flowerHue 28, flowerSat 46, flowerLight 40` for that reason.

**EIGHT SKINS AND TWELVE HAIR COLOURS, AND A LOOK STORES THEM AS INDICES, which is the whole constraint.** `skin` and `hairCol` are positions in `SKINS` and `HAIRS`, so once a gardener can be kept against an account **neither list may ever be reordered again, only appended to**. They WERE reordered when they grew, and that was safe only because nothing was saved yet; `DEFAULT_LOOK` moved with them, to `skin: 1, hairCol: 1`, so the figure a first visitor sees is unchanged.

- **The skins are a RAMP, light to dark, and the four that were there are IN it rather than in front of it.** A swatch grid that is not a ramp reads as a random handful. The four new ones are interleaved: one above `#f6e3cd` at the light end and the rest between the old four.
- **The hairs are eight naturals, dark to light, then FOUR THAT NO HEAD GROWS** (rose, mint, lilac, sky), and the row break falls exactly where the kind changes. The unreal four go LAST because a picker that opens on pink says the gardener is a toy. They are worth having because this is the one choice on the figure with no bearing on anything else, so it is the one place somebody can simply please themselves.
- **Both grids are `av-grid-4`**, so eight and twelve fill two and three clean rows with no orphan tile, checked at 375 and at 1400. No CSS was needed.
- **`garden3d/avatar-kit.js` CARRIES BOTH LISTS AND ITS OWN `DEFAULT_LOOK`, and all three moved together.** The point of a look being data is that one row of numbers draws the flat gardener and the 3D figure, so the moment the two lists disagree one index means two different people. Do not sort, insert into or dedupe either copy on its own.

**THE FLOWER TAKES A CHOSEN HUE, on the project's own rainbow slider.** Both gardens' planter and the bouquet's sender already move a flower's hue that way, so the gardener's does too rather than inventing a third way of choosing a colour. The saturation and lightness stay the SPECIES', so a re-coloured daisy is still a pastel daisy rather than whatever the slider lands on, and picking a different species resets the hue to that flower's own.

**THE TROUSERS ARE THE ONE COLOUR ON THE FIGURE THAT FOLLOWS NOTHING.** They were `hsl(L.top, 16, 38)`, so the legs under any non-bare outfit took the chosen top's hue, and a blue top landed on the Denim tint almost exactly: with Denim wellies on, the figure was one colour from the hem to the sole. `TROUSER` is a fixed stone grey at `hsl(34, 5, 47)`, which is what work trousers are. **AT 5 SATURATION NOTHING IN `TINTS` CAN BE MISTAKEN FOR THEM**: the nearest is Clay at hue 18 and saturation 42. It was 9 for a pass and read faintly tan; measured on the canvas it now renders `rgb(126, 121, 114)` against Denim's `rgb(86, 110, 159)`. **The plain shoe fallback still derives from the top** (`hsl(L.top, 14, 26)`) and may stay there, since it is far darker than the stone and cannot merge with it.

**A BOOT'S SHAFT HALVES ON BARE LEGS.** With shorts on the leg is 52 pixels of skin and a welly's shaft is 54, so it swallowed the whole thing and the shorts appeared to sit straight on the boot. **THE SHOES ALSO GO ON BEFORE THE SHORTS**, so a shaft passes behind the hem rather than over it, which is how a trouser leg covers the top of a boot.

**THE TOP IS MUTED, 26 saturation against the 34 it was**, which puts the clothes just under the gardens' own flowers at 40 to 60 so the figure reads as a person IN a garden rather than a person made of the same stuff as the flowers. Every tone derived from it came down with it.

**NO POCKET UNDER A SKIRT.** `drawTrim` puts one at `b - 40`, which is inside the skirt's own panel, so it came out printed on the front of it.

**THREE COLOURS PER ITEM, not a single fixed one and not the whole palette.** Every garment and accessory carried a hard-coded colour, so a gardener was a figure in their own hue with a fixed blue dungaree and a fixed brown boot stuck on. `TINTS` holds six, each `WEAR` entry names the three that suit it and the one it starts in, and `look.tints` overrides per SLOT.

- **Six denims of a dungaree is a paint chart and three is a choice**, which is why each item names three rather than offering all six.
- **Picking a different item CLEARS the colour**, so a new garment always arrives in the one it was drawn for rather than inheriting whatever the last thing in that slot was changed to.
- **A TILE DRAWS THE ITEM AND THE COLOUR IT IS ASKED FOR.** The tint is stored per SLOT, so `loose` pins the slot to the tile's own id and the caller sets `tints[slot]` for the variant it wants. Without that, every tile in a row repainted itself in whatever the gardener was wearing.

**THE TORSO IS A BELL, not a rounded rectangle, and that is most of what made the figure read as boxy.** It narrows to 58 at the shoulders and flares to 80 at the hips. `bodyPath` draws it and `halfAt(y)` gives its half width at any height, so a hem, a waistband or a cardigan panel can follow the shape rather than being a rectangle laid across it.

- **EVERY GARMENT ON THE TORSO IS DRAWN THROUGH THE BELL'S OWN CLIP** (`inBody`), which keeps a bib or a panel inside the silhouette without any of them having to know the shape. **The skirt is the one exception**, since it flares past the hem on purpose, and it starts at the width the body already is where it begins: started at a fixed width it was a triangle stuck to a torso.
- **THE SHORTS HANG OUT FROM UNDER THE HEM**, two short legs with a turned cuff and bare shins below. Every version drawn ON the torso read as something else: a wide block across the hips is a **nappy**, and its lower third in another tone is a dress with a dark hem. A trouser is a thing that comes out from under a top, so that is where it goes.
- **A COLLAR ON EVERY OUTFIT, and buttons and a pocket on the ones that had no detail of their own.** Almost every garment in both clothing references has a collar and it is the cheapest thing there is for making a flat shape read as clothing. The skirt and the apron take a **frilled hem**, which is the pinafore's.
- **THE SHORTS ARE PAINTED BEFORE THE TORSO**, in `drawUnder`, which is what finally joins them to it: the body's own hem lies over their top. Drawn after the body they sat ON it, and however far up they started the join was a line across the hips.
- **SHORT STUB ARMS off the shoulders**, in the sleeve's own colour (the cardigan's when one is worn, the top's otherwise), ending in a round hand. Two hands alone at the body's edge read as beads stuck on the sides and gave a carried trowel nothing to belong to.

**CLOTHING IS ONE CHOICE, NOT A LAYER PLUS A LEG.** It was "over the clothes" and "on the legs" as two slots, which let somebody build a cardigan with a skirt and shorts under an apron, and most of those combinations looked like an accident rather than an outfit. Six entries, each dressing the whole figure: Plain, Dungarees, Apron, Cardigan, Skirt, Shorts. `bare` says whether the legs are skin below it.

- **Plain IS the none**, so `NO_NONE` covers the clothes slot: an empty clothes slot would draw a gardener with nothing on below the waist.
- **A clothing tile draws the whole lower figure**, body and legs and outfit, so Plain is a picture too. A body and a pair of trousers is what "no outfit" looks like; an empty tile is not.
- **A row is three columns when its count divides by three and four otherwise**, so no row ever ends in an orphan tile: six outfits fall 3 and 3, four feet fall 4, three fall 3.

**FIVE SLOTS: clothes, neck, feet, hold, face.** `STEP_SLOTS` is the one place the split between the two steps is written: Clothes takes the hue and the outfit, Carrying takes the other four. **There is no glove slot**, built and removed in one pass: at this size a glove is a hand in another colour, which is a choice nobody can see.

**WHAT IS CARRIED HANGS FROM THE FIST.** Its handle runs vertically THROUGH the middle of the hand and the object hangs below, which is how a can and a trowel are actually carried and what stops them floating beside the arm. The order is the other half of it: the object is painted, then the palm over its handle, then a thumb across the handle, so the grip is a thing you can see.

- **That reorder is what let the trowel be fixed.** It was a thin stick with a grey head off to one side, which is a **paintbrush**, because a narrow handle longer than its head reads as one whatever the head is. It has a fat short handle, a ferrule and a broad scoop with a crease down it.
- **A held item's TILE draws one hand, not two.** Fitted, a pair spans eighty pixels of empty space with a can in the middle, so the tile came out as a small object between two pale dots.

**THE SPROUT HAS NO COWL. A stem out of the crown and nothing else.** There was a cream cowl round the face as well, to keep all eight species in one silhouette family. Once the flower moved in front of the body that cowl covered the shoulders and the whole top of the torso came out cream, so the tulip and the lavender were the only two that did not leave the body intact. The reference characters have no cowl either.

**ACCESSORY REDRAWS, and every one was the same fault: a piece that leaned on the figure around it to be named turned into something else on its own tile.** The scarf was a mallet, then a hook, then a key before it became a band with a knot and two fringed tails hanging from the MIDDLE; every failure hung the ends off one edge, so the picture had a head and a handle. The satchel was a ladle until its strap curved and its bag gained a flap and a buckle, because a straight handle ending in a bowl is a spoon whatever colour it is. The dungarees were the apron again until they got a leg split and two buttoned braces. Two blocks are two blocks and not boots, so those got a shaft, a cuff, a foot and a sole. Two beige pills read as pebbles, so the clogs got a wooden sole and a rounded toe. A plain trapezoid is a shape rather than a garment, so the apron got two neck straps, a waist tie and a pocket. The cardigan is **open down the middle**, which is the whole of what a cardigan is. And the shorts were one band across the hips, which reads as underwear: they are two short legs with a hem and a split up the middle.

**EVERY PICKER TILE DRAWS THE REAL THING, through `AvatarDraw.icon`.** The steps were four rows reading Scarf, Satchel, None and Dungarees, Apron, None: eleven words and no pictures, on a page whose whole subject is how somebody looks.

- **EVERY ICON IS FITTED, NOT DRAWN TO A BOX.** The piece is painted alone at figure size into one scratch canvas, its painted pixels are measured, and exactly that box is scaled into the tile at 88 percent. That is the bouquet's `fitThumb` rule and it is what makes a trowel, a pair of boots and a whole lavender spike read at one weight; sized by their own boxes the trowel is a speck beside the dungarees. It is also what fixed the FLOWER tiles, which drew at a flat `px * 0.34` and so gave the lavender, which grows upward out of its origin, a quarter of the size the round blooms got.
- **It goes through the very routines that put the piece on the gardener**, so a tile can never show one thing and choosing it do another.
- **COLOURS STAY AS SWATCHES.** Skin and hair colour are squares of the colour, because a swatch already IS a picture of a colour and four faces in four skins say nothing a square of the skin does not. What gets a picture is anything told apart by its SHAPE: the hair styles, the clothes (the body's own silhouette in that hue rather than a square of it), the eight accessories, the eight flowers.
