- **THREE ACCESSORIES HAD TO BE REDRAWN, because they leaned on the figure around them to be named.** On its own tile the scarf was a band with one tab hanging off it and read as a **mallet**; the satchel was a straight thin strap ending in a small box and read as a **ladle**, which is the shape of a spoon whatever colour it is; and the dungarees were one rounded panel with two short straps, which was the same picture as the apron in the tile beside it, and a picker may not show the same picture twice.
  - **The scarf took three goes and each failure was the same fault.** Both tails off one side read as a coat HOOK; an outlined ring with the tails off it read as a KEY. All three hung the ends off one edge, so the picture had a head and a handle. It is a band, a knot, and two fringed tails hanging from the MIDDLE now: symmetrical, and a symmetrical thing with fringe on the bottom is a scarf.
  - **The satchel's strap is a CURVE and its bag is large**, with a flap and a buckle. The buckle is what separates a satchel from a lunch box at this size.
  - **The dungarees got a leg split and two buttoned braces.** That is what tells them from an apron, not the colour.
- **`drawFace` was lifted out of `paint`** so a hair tile can draw a real head rather than a coloured shape nobody can name.
- **FOUR ROWS OF PICTURES IS A TALLER STEP THAN FOUR ROWS OF WORDS.** The Carrying step grew about 230px the moment the tiles gained art and ran its Back and Continue off the foot of a 1000px window. The pictures stay and the box around each one is tightened instead, `.av-grid-wear` at 38px icons with 7px padding, which is the cheaper of the two. Checked after at 375: no sideways scroll and tiles 98px.
- **THE FLOWER NOTE'S TEST IS `=== "sprout"`, and it used to be `!== "ring"`.** No species has ever been called `ring`, so the line fired on all eight and told somebody picking a rose that a rose has no ring of petals to sit inside.

- **With a hood on, hair is a FRINGE and little else**, clipped to the face so it can never spill over the petals behind it. The three that were there before are gone: "tucked" read as a headband and "long" as a helmet clamped over the face.
- **The earlier keepsake system is recorded here because the reasoning still holds if it comes back**: one keepsake per species, earned by planting, locked rather than hidden so a tile says which flower opens it, and read from `personal_gratitude_garden` in this browser.

- **Tapping the accessory already on takes it off**, so a slot is never a trap.
- **THE PHONE IS A FLEX COLUMN WITH `order`, NOT `display: block`, and the reason is that the figure and the tabs have to STICK.** In source order the rail comes first, which is right on a laptop where it is a left rail; on a phone the picture belongs on top, and only a flex column lets the two swap without moving them in the DOM. The stage sticks at `top: 54px`, below the corner icons, and the rail directly under it at `calc(54px + var(--av-stage-h))`. Left to scroll, choosing a shoe means scrolling the gardener off the top and back again for every tap, which is what the bouquet's phone layout was rebuilt three times to stop.
- **The Keep bar is fixed to the SCREEN, not to the studio**, which is why the studio may never carry a transform: a transformed ancestor becomes the containing block for `position: fixed` and the bar would land at the foot of the studio instead. Same trap the bouquet's Done bar already carries.
- **The nameplate under the figure is hidden on a phone.** On a laptop it is a caption with the field below it; in the phone's row layout the two sit side by side and it is the same word twice.
- **The house SVG has to be IN the markup.** `swapHomeIcon` in `garden-cursor.js` REPLACES an existing svg inside `#back-to-home` and returns if it finds none, so a text only Home becomes an empty 34px circle on a phone, where the shared rule shrinks the pill to a circle and the word has nowhere to go.
- The phone block is LAST in the stylesheet, like the project's other four, since a later rule beats an earlier one at equal specificity.

## The Guest Book (`guestbook/`)

**A FIFTH PAGE, and the only one on the site that genuinely cannot work without an account.** Everything else here keeps running with no database configured; this one says so in a line and stops, because a guest book with no way to tell one signer from another is not a guest book.

A card is somebody's gardener, the four things they said about themselves, one line in their own words, and the date. **Anybody can READ it. Signing needs an account**, which is what makes one entry belong to one person and lets them come back and change it.

- **THE TABLE HOLDS ALMOST NOTHING: the signer, their note and when.** The name, the gardener and the four answers come from `profiles`, which `garden-account.js` already selects in two places for the friends list. So a card UPDATES when somebody changes their gardener, rather than being a snapshot going quietly stale, and the whole feature costs one small table.
- **ONE PERSON, ONE ENTRY, ENFORCED BY A UNIQUE INDEX** rather than by the page, because anybody can edit the page in their own browser. It is also what the upsert conflicts against, so without it signing twice would leave two cards.
- **THREE WRITE POLICIES, NOT ONE `for all`.** Insert checks the row going IN and update and delete check the row already there, and a single policy cannot say both.
- **`select("*")`, NEVER A LIST OF COLUMNS.** Supabase answers a query naming a missing column with a 400 and an error OBJECT rather than throwing, so on a project where Step 10 has not been run, naming one would empty the result and take every entry down with it.
- **AN EMPTY LIST AND A FAILED QUERY MUST NOT SAY THE SAME SENTENCE, and they did.** `load` set the "not set up yet" message and then `draw` ran with no rows and replaced it with "Nobody has signed it yet", so a project missing the table looked exactly like a project nobody had signed. `draw(quiet)` is the fix. **Anything that reports a state after an error has to know an error happened.**
- **THE MESSAGE NAMES STEP 10 ONLY WHEN THE TABLE IS REALLY MISSING.** A permission refusal gets a different sentence, and the database's own message is always shown rather than a guess about it, which is the rule the comments panel learned the hard way: a message that tells somebody to go and run SQL has to be right, because they will go and run it.
- **PROFILES ARE A SECOND QUERY, not an embedded join.** A PostgREST join would need a foreign key from `guestbook` straight to `profiles`, and this schema points at `auth.users`, so asking for one would be a guess about somebody else's database.
- **A SIGNER WITH NO GARDENER GETS THEIR INITIAL, never a default figure**, which is `avatarFor`'s rule in the friends list: a stock gardener would make everybody who has not built one appear to have made the same person.
- **YOUR OWN ENTRY IS AT THE TOP, not the bottom.** Signing is what you came to do, and a page that grows would put it further away every week.
- **NO USERNAME, NO ENTRY.** A card with nothing under the picture is worse than no card, and the account module already owns the screen that claims a name.
- **THE GUEST BOOK'S LINE IS ITS OWN**, separate from the about-you note that travels with the gardener everywhere. This one was written for this page and the people reading it.
- Step 9 should be run first: without the `look` column every card falls back to an initial, which works and looks like nobody has built a gardener.

## `garden3d/`, a SEPARATE exploration, not part of the site

**Nothing in `garden3d/` is loaded by the four pages, and nothing in the four pages loads it.** It is a spike for a possible thesis direction: the same garden as a cosy low poly, avatar based, isometric 3D space, after Animal Crossing and Cozy Island. **The web version stays exactly as it is.** If that ever stops being true, this note is wrong.

- **It answers ONE question**: at the distance the game would be played at, does each species read as itself? Everything in it exists to test that and nothing else. There is no avatar system, no persistence, no interaction beyond the controls.
- **Three.js r169 is VENDORED** at `garden3d/lib/three.module.min.js`, 687KB, imported as a plain ES module. No build step, no CDN at runtime, which is the same rule the rest of the project follows.
- **No model files, ever.** `flower-kit.js` holds five primitives (petal, stem, spike, disc, and the merge helper) and a parameter row per species. That is what keeps the person's chosen HUE working, exactly as in 2D, and it reuses the hues and petal counts `SPECIES` already carries in `bouquet-sketch.js`.
- **PITCH is the one parameter the 2D version does not have**: how far a petal leans from vertical. Tulip 8 degrees stays a closed cup, daisy 78 is flat, lily 100 is swept back. Pitch does most of the work of telling them apart.
- **THE SILHOUETTE TEST EARNED ITS PLACE IMMEDIATELY.** With colour removed, the daisy and the sunflower were the same shape: a ring of petals with a centre, differing only in size. The same failure the chrysanthemum had in 2D. The fix was not petal count, it was HABIT: the daisy is a clump of three low heads, and the sunflower is one tall stem with a big disc that NODS forward 24 degrees. Nodding is also the hook for the behaviour idea, a head that follows the sun.
- **The growth timings are the web version's, unchanged**: `GROW_RISE` 3400ms, `GROW_OPEN` 1700ms, a cubic ease out on the rise and a smoothstep on the opening, the bloom scaling from 0.18. A flower grows at the same speed in both versions, which is continuity rather than coincidence.
- **All interface is HTML over the canvas**, and that is the architecture the real thing would use: never build a text field in WebGL, since the reflection writing has to stay accessible and keep the project's own type.
- Measured: 60fps at 1400 by 900 with all eight species, fourteen plants and a pond, 704KB on disk including three.js.

**ALL EIGHT ARE IN NOW, and the last three are the ones that taught the most.**

- **RINGS are the second assembly, and the rose and the lotus are one routine with different numbers.** `addRings` builds a bloom as concentric rings of petals whose pitch climbs from nearly closed at the middle to past flat at the outside, each ring turned half a step off the one inside it so petals fall into gaps rather than stacking into columns. That the two hardest blooms came out of one routine is the kit doing its job.
- **A ROSE PETAL IS ALMOST AS WIDE AS IT IS LONG, and getting that wrong is what made the first three attempts a hollyhock.** Narrow petals at climbing pitches spiral upward into a cone of frills. Wide ones overlap sideways and close into a ball. Each ring also sits a little LOWER than the one inside it; stacked at one height the climbing pitches read as a staircase.
- **The rose's outer ring is pushed PAST flat, to 94 degrees**, so the ball sits on a flange of splayed petals. In silhouette a plain ball of petals is a tulip bud drawn rounder, and the flange is what tells the two apart. It is also a spray of two heads, the same habit fix the daisy took.
- **(HISTORY) THE SAKURA WAS A TREE, THEN A STEM FLOWER, AND IS NOW GONE.** It is kept here because the NOTCHED PETAL it forced into the kit is still there and still useful: `petalGeometry` takes `tip: "notch"` alongside round and point. As one blossom on a stem with ordinary petals a sakura is a five petalled pink flower, which is a daisy in another colour, and the cleft tip is what rescued it. The poppy that replaced it needs no such rescue.
- **THE TREE IS GONE FROM BOTH SCENES.** `buildTree` and `TREE` are still in `flower-kit.js` and nothing calls them; they are kept because a canopy is reusable scenery, not because anything depends on them. **Neither scene imports `buildTree` any more**, so do not assume from the export that a tree is on screen.
- **A canopy is many small blobs, all at the SAME detail.** One sphere is a lollipop. Sixteen icosahedra at radius 0.1 to 0.17 give a bumpy edge that reads as blossom. Mixing a detail 0 with a detail 1 put three hard crystals in a canopy of soft ones, which reads as a fault rather than as variety; the depth comes from two tones instead.
- **(HISTORY) THE LOTUS GREW ON WATER, THEN ON A STEM, AND IS NOW GONE**, replaced by the peony. The pond went with it. What the episode proved is worth keeping: **a species that needs its own kind of ground is a rule every other part of the system then has to carry**, and eight species that all grow the same way is worth more than two that are botanically correct.
- **The pads are gone from the plantable path but the routine is worth remembering**: a green disc becomes a lily pad when a wedge is left out of it, a `CylinderGeometry` with a `thetaLength` of 1.83 PI.
- **BOTANICAL HONESTY IS NOT THE POINT, PLANTABILITY IS.** A lotus is aquatic and a cherry blossom grows on a branch, and both were modelled that way first because that is what they are. The cost landed somewhere else: every other part of the system had to learn about ground types, spacing per species, a sapling scale, a pool per slot. Eight species that all grow the same way is worth more than two that are correct.
- **Every petal material is registered with its own lightness offset** (`userData.tint`), so one hue slider moves a rose's five rings together and keeps the depth between them. A single `petalMat` could not have held a bloom built from rings.
- **The silhouette test passes at garden and wide distance with all eight**, and the two new plant types are the easiest of the lot to name: a tree is a tree, and pads are pads.

**THE LOOP IS IN: reflect, choose a spot, watch it grow.** The species test asks whether you can tell them apart; this asks the question that comes after it, whether PLANTING one feels calm. Three things make it a loop rather than a placement tool, and each earned its place.

- **The same three questions and the same weighted vote.** Rating 4, what shaped the day 3, two per keyword hit in the sentence, `chooseSpecies` in `spike.js`. **The sentence is not optional decoration: without it the rating always wins and the second question is a button that changes nothing**, since 4 beats 3 on its own. Verified in the page: Okay plus People alone gives a sakura, and adding "love my family" turns it into a rose.
- **WHERE a thing may grow is ONE RULE now, and that is the whole of what the special cases were worth.** `PLOT` held a row per species: the water for the lotus, open grass for the tree, the bed for the rest, each with its own spacing and its own distance for the avatar to stand back. With every species in the same soil it is one `BED` row. What survives is the part that was always doing the work: a flower needs ROOM, and a refusal says so in words, because a marker that simply will not turn green is a puzzle rather than an answer.
- **The spacing rule used to take the AVERAGE of the two gaps, never the larger**, and that is worth keeping in mind if a species ever needs its own footprint again. A tree asked 1.3 of another tree and a daisy 0.3 of another daisy; taking the larger made a tree demand its own 1.3 from every daisy in the bed beside it, which left the open grass unplantable for the one species that had to go there.
- **THE BED MUST NOT BE FULL.** The two specimen rows filled it exactly, so "pick a spot" became a hunt for the one legal pixel. The bed is deeper now and the rows sit back, leaving a free strip along the front. **A garden with no room in it cannot be planted in, which is obvious in hindsight and was not obvious while the bed was a test rig.**
- **The avatar WALKS there, and stops on the FAR side of the spot from the camera.** Stopping short along the walking line put it between the camera and the flower it had just planted, hiding the one thing the person was waiting to see. It takes the camera's current angle, so this holds at all four turns, and it is pushed out of the pond if the far side of a lotus is water.
- **The flower is created at the CLICK with its birth set in the FUTURE**, `now + travel`, so it waits in the ground until somebody is there to plant it. `growth()` already drew a flower whose moment had not come, so nothing needed to change for that.
- **A loop with no end just stops.** The closing line is timed to the frame the bloom finishes opening, at `born + GROW_RISE`, and says the species and its meaning.
- Measured: 60fps at 1400 by 900 with the plot, the pond, the tree and sixteen plants.
- **What the loop has NOT answered yet**: a tree grows to full height in the same 3.4 seconds as a daisy, which is the web version's timing used where it does not belong.

**THE AVATAR, AND CUSTOMISATION TIED TO REFLECTION (`avatar-kit.js`).** The capsule stand in is gone. The avatar is built the same way the flowers are, from primitives and a parameter row, so **a look is DATA**: a row of numbers survives being saved against an account, sent to a shared garden and read back next year, where a mesh does not.

- **THE WARDROBE IS FILLED BY PLANTING, NOT BY A SHOP.** Every keepsake belongs to one species: plant a daisy and you keep a daisy chain, plant a sunflower and you keep the sun hat. Nothing is bought, nothing is timed and nothing can be missed, which is the difference between a reward and a record. At the end of a month the avatar is wearing an account of what the person noticed. A locked tile says which flower unlocks it, so the wardrobe doubles as a reason to come back without ever nagging.
- **A keepsake is worn straight away ONLY if that slot is empty**, never over something already chosen. A gift that silently takes off what you were wearing is not a gift. Tapping a worn keepsake takes it off, so a slot is never a trap.
- **The keepsakes ARE the flowers.** `buildBloom` was extracted out of `buildFlower` so a daisy in the bed and a daisy on somebody's head are one piece of code. That is the kit paying for itself a second time: the 2D version pays for the same flower maths in seven places, and the obvious way to build a flower crown would have made an eighth.
- **What is worn is the FLOWER, never the HABIT**, and that rule paid for itself twice. While the sakura was a tree, `buildBloom` carried an override giving it a five petalled wearable row rather than putting a small tree on somebody's head; when the species went back to being a stem flower the override simply went. The lotus was carried on its own lily pad, which was the one keepsake that was not simply the flower, and it went with the species: `cupped` is deleted and the peony is carried as a posy through `held`, the routine the lily and the tulip already use.
- **Three slots, head, shoulders and held, so three can be worn at once** and no two keepsakes ever fight for the same place. Eight keepsakes across three slots is a wardrobe; eight in one slot is a list.
- **A BRIM IS MEASURED AGAINST THE HEAD.** The sun hat was drawn at 0.44 against a head of 0.23, which is what a sun hat is, and from the only angle this game has, above, it read as a mushroom and swallowed the shoulders. It is 0.33.
- **The arms exist for the keepsakes, not for the figure**, since three of the eight are carried and a held flower with no hand under it floats beside the body like a bug. They are a SLEEVE and a HAND, not one bare capsule: in the skin tone they read as two pale blocks stuck to a jumper.
- **The eyes are not decoration.** With a blank head there is no telling which way the avatar is facing, so turning to face the camera in the wardrobe, and turning toward a flower after planting, both said nothing at all. They also have to sit BELOW the hairline, or the hair cap swallows them.
- **The wardrobe brings the camera to the avatar AND DROPS ITS PITCH.** Choosing a hat while the wearer is thirty pixels tall across the garden is choosing blind, and the garden is read from above where a head is mostly hair. The angle round the scene never changes, only the target, the width and the height, so it is the same world seen closer rather than a second camera with its own rules.
- **"Unlock everything" is a TEST control and says so on the button.** The spike keeps nothing between reloads, so without it the wardrobe can only be seen by planting eight flowers again. It is not part of the design.
- **A measurement trap worth keeping**: the browser pane throttles `requestAnimationFrame` while it is not painting, so an fps sample taken straight after a tool call reported 12. Taken after a screenshot, which forces a paint, the same scene reports 60. Force a frame before measuring, the same way p5 needs `redraw()` before a pixel sample.

**THE SHARED GARDEN IS A SECOND SCENE, `shared.html` and `shared-scene.js`, AND IT HAS NO AVATARS.** The 2D page is one room a day, keyed on the date, that anybody can plant one flower in with a typed name and a sentence. `garden3d/index.html` is your own plot; this is the day everyone is in.

- **No avatars, and that is the design rather than a saving.** A private plot has one person in it who walks around. A shared day has twenty strangers who were each here for a minute, and twenty avatars standing about in a field is a lobby. What is left of somebody is their flower, which is what the 2D page already says.
- **ONE THING IN THE MIDDLE, AND EVERYONE PLANTS AROUND IT.** A field with no centre reads as a grid of strangers; a circle reads as people who turned up to the same place. It was a cherry tree while the sakura was a species, then briefly a cairn. It is a **WOODEN SIGN CARRYING THE DATE**, and that earns the middle rather than just filling it: **the middle of a shared garden should not be one person's flower**, and this garden IS one day, filling from morning to night and emptying at midnight. The page heading says "Today's garden"; the sign says which today. `buildCairn` is still exported and unused, like `buildTree`.
- **THE RINGS ARE THE DAY.** Slots fill outward in the order people arrived, so the first person of the morning stands nearest the tree and the last stands at the edge, and **the camera widens as the day fills** (`fitted()`): an empty morning is a small garden and a full evening is a wide one. The four rings hold 56 people.
- **EVERYBODY GETS THE SAME AMOUNT OF ROOM, and now nothing has to be bent to give it to them.** This carried two exceptions while the sakura was a tree and the lotus grew on water: a sakura in a ring slot swallowed its neighbours and the landmark in the middle, so it was shrunk to a sapling at 0.58, and a lotus brought a pool of water the size of its own slot. **One person's choice cannot be allowed to take three slots**, which is a rule a shared garden needs and a private plot does not, and with every species a stem flower it needs no code at all. The tree in the middle is `buildTree`, scenery rather than somebody's sakura.
- **The names are HTML tags positioned by projecting each bloom's own world point**, the same rule as every other piece of text here: the project's type, readable by a screen reader, crisp at any distance, and no font texture. **Only the hovered flower's name is shown, plus your own always**, because twenty names at once is a field of labels with a garden somewhere behind it.
- **The card sits BESIDE the flower, on the side away from the middle, and it took two wrong placements to get there.** Anchored above and clamped, it came to rest on the title card in the corner. Flipped below, it covered the very flower it was describing, which is worse. Beside it can do neither, and putting it on the outward side keeps the tree and the early rings visible while one flower's line is read.
- **`growth.js` is a module now, and that is the 2D lesson applied before it cost anything.** The web project carries the growth sequence twice, character for character, with a note saying to edit one and edit the other. There are two scenes here, so the same crack was about to open; `GROW_RISE`, `GROW_OPEN`, the two easings and `growFlower` live in one file that both import. **`growFlower` writes the whole scale**, so a sapling has to be told its size again each frame, which is the cost of that and is worth it.
- **One flower a day, and the button SAYS so rather than disappearing**: a control that vanishes reads as a fault.
- **NOTHING HERE IS NETWORKED.** The day is fourteen seeded entries, invented for the spike, so a shared garden can be looked at while there is nobody to share it with. The real thing keeps the split the 2D page already has: the living room in p5.party, and likes and comments in Supabase. The like button toggles locally only; one like per person stays a database rule, not a page rule.
- Measured: 60fps, and the two pages link to each other.

**THE POPPY AND THE PEONY REPLACE THE SAKURA AND THE LOTUS HERE TOO, AND THE ISLAND GAINED ITS OWN SCENERY.** The 3D spike now carries the web version's eight exactly: tulip, rose, sunflower, poppy, lily, daisy, peony, lavender.

- **THE POPPY IS THE SET'S ONLY OPEN BOWL, AND ITS BLACK EYE IS THE SPECIES.** Six petals where the daisy has twelve, each nearly as wide as it is long. It took two corrections that are worth recording. At pitch 48 those six wide petals overlapped into a continuous flat disc and the flower read as **a dinner plate on a stem**; at 38 they stand as walls, which is what a poppy does and what makes it the only cup in a set of flat rings and recurves. And the eye was a dot at 0.042 against petals of 0.16, invisible from the garden camera: it is 0.07, domed, and LIFTED clear of the cupped petals by a new `centre.y`, because at cup 0.68 the petals rose past a disc sitting on the origin and swallowed it. `stamenTone` is also new, since the kit's default gold stamens filled the black eye back in.
- **THE PEONY IS THE OTHER RINGED BLOOM, so it has to differ from the rose in HABIT as well as size.** The rose is a tight spray of TWO small heads; the peony is ONE big loose dome, five rings wider at every step with the outer two pushed past flat so it spreads into a flange rather than closing into a ball, and paler. It replaced the lotus, which was already the same `addRings` routine, so the kit needed nothing new for it.
- **Checked at the GARDEN's own angle, not a three-quarter view.** A species sheet rendered at the low angle read the poppy as a plate and was wrong about it; at about 42 degrees down, which is what the camera actually does, all eight separate.
- **The keepsakes follow the species**: a Poppy pin at the head and a Peony posy carried. `cupped` is deleted with the lotus.

**THE LIGHT WAS WASHED OUT, AND IT WAS THREE THINGS AT ONCE RATHER THAN A SHORTAGE OF LIGHT.** The hour has not moved; it is the same late afternoon, lit properly.

- **There was no TONE MAPPING at all**, so every value went to the screen raw and the bright half of the picture sat flat against its own ceiling. `setupRenderer` sets ACES at 1.18 exposure, in one place so the two scenes cannot drift.
- **The fill was drowning the key.** A hemisphere light at 1.05 against a sun of 1.55 is most of the light arriving from everywhere, and light from everywhere is exactly what removes form from flat shading, which has nothing but the angle between a face and the key to work with. The ratio went from about 1.5 to 1 up to about 12 to 1.
- **THE AMBIENT IS AN ENVIRONMENT NOW, NOT A HEMISPHERE LIGHT**, and that is what pays for moving every material from Lambert to Standard. A HemisphereLight is two colours blended by how far a normal points up; a real environment carries the sky down one side of a petal and the grass up the other, so a white bloom picks up green from the ground it stands on. `skyEnvironment` GENERATES it, a 32 by 64 gradient through `PMREMGenerator`, so no file is added.
- **The shadow side then went nearly black**, which is a night look rather than a late afternoon one. Hemisphere 0.3 against `environmentIntensity` 0.8 keeps the contrast and puts colour back in the dark half.
- **The fog began at 23 with the camera 16 out**, so the hills it was meant to soften were half dissolved. 30 to 62 leaves it on the rim.
- **Measured after, on the spike's own load** (sixteen plants, the props, the island): 139 draw calls, 18.3k triangles, 0.6ms of CPU per frame against a 16.7ms budget at 60fps. **fps itself cannot be read in the browser pane**, which throttles `requestAnimationFrame` to single figures when it is not painting; time explicit `render()` calls instead.
- **THE FIGURES IN THIS BLOCK WERE THEN SUPERSEDED** by the cute pass below, which lifted the light again and changed the tone mapper. The reasoning holds; the numbers do not.

**THE ISLAND HAS ITS OWN SCENERY, and none of it belongs to a species.** The tree and the pond were built because the sakura needed a canopy and the lotus needed water, so when the species went they were props with no part left to play. `world.js` exports `buildRock`, `buildShrub`, `buildBench`, `buildPath` and `buildCairn`, all from the same primitives the flowers use, all flat shaded, none tied to a flower. **Nothing here has to change the next time the eight do.** The bench is the one prop that is about the subject rather than the scene: a garden you reflect in should have somewhere to do it from.

**NO TWO PLANTS STAND THE SAME WAY, and the size has to go on an INNER group.** `buildFlower` seeded its generators 7 and 11, FIXED, so a row of eight daisies was one daisy drawn eight times and a bed read as a rack. It takes a `seed` now and every caller passes one, so a reload still gives the same garden. `vary()` turns each plant to face anywhere, leans it a few degrees and scales it about seven percent. **The scale cannot go on the returned group**: `growFlower` writes the whole of the outer scale every frame as `(1, up, 1)`, so a per plant scale there is wiped on the first frame of growth and the bed snaps back to uniform the moment anything grows. Rotation is safe, since growth never touches it.

**THE CUTE PASS: SMOOTH, BRIGHT, AND ROUND.** The reference was a storybook illustration of plants on little blocks of earth, and the brief was Animal Crossing rather than the outlined storybook look, so there are no outlines: it is smooth shading, saturated colour and chunky form.

- **"SQUARE" WAS NEVER THE POLYGON COUNT. It was `flatShading: true` on every material**, which draws one normal per triangle so every facet is a hard plane by definition. It is off everywhere now.
- **TURNING IT OFF ON ITS OWN DID ALMOST NOTHING, and that is the trap worth remembering.** Geometry built by hand here is a soup of loose triangles, so `computeVertexNormals` has nothing to average: each triangle owns its three vertices and gets one flat normal, which is what flat shading was doing anyway. `weldSmooth` in `flower-kit.js` welds by position first, at a 0.1mm tolerance, so a vertex gets its neighbours back and the normal becomes the average of every face meeting there. **Anything new that builds geometry from raw positions must go through `fromPositions`, which welds.**
- **THE PETAL WAS A FOLDED STRIP, three vertices across**, so its cup was a literal crease down the middle that no shading could round off. It is an 8 by 4 grid carrying a parabolic cup: 64 triangles a petal against 24. That is the cheapest place in the scene to spend geometry, since the petals are what anybody looks at.
- **`flatShading` IS SAFE TO REMOVE from three's own primitives** (Box, Cylinder, Plane, Sphere) because they already split their vertices per face where an edge should be hard. It is NOT safe on `IcosahedronGeometry`, which three builds non-indexed, so it stays faceted whatever the material says. The rocks, the shrub blobs and the cairn stones are low segment SPHERES now, jittered for the rocks, which is what turned them from broken shards into pebbles.
- **NEUTRAL TONE MAPPING, NOT ACES, and this is most of the palette.** ACES is a film curve that desaturates as it rolls off, which is right for photographic material and quietly greys out exactly the saturated greens and pinks this look is made of. `THREE.NeutralToneMapping` keeps hue and saturation and only compresses the top end, so what is in the palette is what appears. It is guarded with an ACES fallback, being a newer addition to three than the rest of the file.
- **THE ISLAND IS A BLOCK OF EARTH, NOT A DISC**, which is the single thing that makes the reference's little plots read as cute: you can see how thick the ground is. The wall FOLLOWS THE LAND'S OWN RIM rather than being a plain cylinder, because that rim undulates by up to a unit and a cylinder at a fixed height would stand proud on one side and leave a gap on the other. It reads those vertices back out of the geometry, which is why the y and z look swapped where it is built.
- **The light went up again**: exposure 1.15 under the neutral curve, sun 2.3 at a whiter `0xfff0cf`, hemisphere 0.5, `environmentIntensity` 1.05, fog 34 to 70, and the sky and grass pushed toward the reference's saturation. The hour is still afternoon; it is a bright one now.
- **A DUPLICATE `const tri` BLANKED THE WHOLE SCENE**, with a syntax error that my own probe missed because it attached its `error` listener after `await`ing the page load. **Read the console rather than trusting a listener you attached late**, and note that this pane's console buffer SURVIVES navigation, so a fixed error goes on being reported: confirm against a live `import()` instead.

**WEBXR LIGHTING ESTIMATION, `garden3d/xr-light.js`, asked for by the lecturer.** In an `immersive-ar` session the device reads the room through its camera and returns, per frame, nine spherical harmonic coefficients per channel (the ambient light of the room as a low frequency function of direction) and a primary light direction and intensity (the brightest source in it). So a flower on somebody's table picks up the colour of their curtains.

- **IT CANNOT BE THE ONLY LIGHTING, and that decides where it belongs.** It runs ONLY inside an active AR session on supporting hardware, today an ARCore Android phone or a passthrough headset. On a laptop `isSessionSupported` answers false, the button stays hidden and none of it runs. `world.js` still lights the garden for everybody else and the module restores every value on the way out.
- **THE SCENE IS SHRUNK AND PUSHED AWAY ON ENTRY.** The island is about fifteen units across and in AR one unit is one METRE, so entering without scaling drops a fifteen metre garden through the floor and around the user. It becomes a tabletop object a little over a metre wide, 1.5m in front, and the scale, position, sky, fog and environment are all saved and put back when the session ends.
- **The sky, the fog and the generated environment all go OFF in AR.** A painted sky and a fog fading to it would both draw over the room, and the point of the exercise is that the real room supplies the light.
- **The primary light's colour is normalised against its peak channel** and the magnitude handed to `intensity`, or a bright room blows every colour out to white.
- **`setAnimationLoop`, NOT `requestAnimationFrame`.** Inside an XR session the device drives the frames and a plain rAF loop simply stops being called; outside one three uses rAF for it anyway, so nothing changes for anybody else. It is also the only way to get the `XRFrame`, which is what a light estimate is read from.
- **I COULD NOT TEST THIS ON HARDWARE.** The browser pane reports no AR support, so what is verified is that the module loads, the page runs unchanged without it, and the button stays hidden. The estimation path itself needs a real device.

**EVERYTHING IS ROUND NOW, and the facets went both ways, so here is the history in one place so it is not flipped again.** Smooth first, then faceted for the low poly farm reference, and now smooth for good on the lecturer's note. `flatShading` is off everywhere.

- **An icosahedron cannot be smoothed.** three builds `IcosahedronGeometry` non-indexed, so it reads as flat planes whatever the material says. Rocks, shrubs and cairn stones are low segment SPHERES, jittered; that is the only reason they changed shape rather than just shading.
- **`roundedBox` is what every wooden thing uses.** A `BoxGeometry` is six planes meeting at right angles and a right angle catches a hard specular line down every edge. It is a rounded rectangle extruded with a bevel, so all twelve edges come off at once, using `THREE.Shape` and `ExtrudeGeometry`, both core. **It also gives the sign board its two material groups** (caps and rim) where a box needed six materials in a fixed order.
- **`discGeometry` IS A DOME, NOT A DRUM.** It was a cylinder, so a daisy's centre met its own side at a right angle and caught a hard line right in the middle of the picture. A squashed sphere has no edge anywhere. The lavender's buds and the stamen tips were octahedra, which read as crystals, and are spheres.
- **SHADOW QUALITY IS TEXEL DENSITY, not map size.** 2048 over a 14 by 14 box is 146 texels per unit, and most of that box was empty island. 4096 over 11 is 372, and an edge that is resolved can then be softened on purpose rather than by accident. `normalBias` is what keeps that softness off the object casting it, or it creeps up as a grey rind.
- **THREE WOODS, NOT TEN.** Every wooden thing had picked its own brown as it was built, all within a few degrees of each other: close enough to look like a mistake, far enough apart that the bed, fence and bench never matched. One board, one post, one dark trim.

**BORROWING FROM A REALISTIC PACK WITHOUT BECOMING ONE.** A Sketchfab low poly flower pack was offered as reference. It pulls the OPPOSITE way from the four rounds before it: naturalistic proportions, pointed tips, strappy foliage. The decision was to take details and keep the cute clay direction, and to build them rather than download the pack, for three reasons worth restating: no asset files anywhere, every flower generated from a parameter row, and **the planter's chosen hue flowing through all of it, which a model with baked colours cannot do.**

- **FOLIAGE FROM THE GROUND is what stopped them reading as lollipops.** Every flower in that reference has blades rising out of the earth, not just leaves clipped to a stem, and ours had none at all. Two to four long arching blades per plant, under the small stem leaves rather than instead of them. The single biggest gain of the pass.
- **THINNER STEMS THAT LEAN.** 0.012 was about a fifth of a daisy's bloom and read as a dowel with a flower on it, which is much of what "clunky" meant. It is 0.0085, and the per head bend runs wider so a spray crosses itself rather than standing as parallel rods.
- **VEINING, PAINTED IN CODE.** The richness of that reference is mostly texture. It is drawn to a canvas once, the way the wooden sign draws its date, so no file ships. **IT IS A MASK, NOT A COLOUR**: three multiplies `map` by `color`, so a white sheet with darker lines says only WHERE it is darker and lets the chosen hue supply what colour that is. A painted texture would have baked one flower's colour into every flower. It can only ever DARKEN, since white is as bright as a canvas goes, so the base is pure white and the veins are faint over it, averaging about 0.97.
- **THIS NEEDED UVs, which the hand built geometry had never had.** `petalGeometry` emits them with u across the petal and v along it, so vertical bands land as streaks running base to tip, and front and back share them so a marking reads through. `weldSmooth` and `mergeGeometries` both had to learn to carry them, the same lesson as the vertex colours before: **anything added to a vertex has to be taught to both of those or it silently vanishes.** The merge defaults missing UVs to 0,0, which the texture is painted plain at, so a part without its own UVs picks up no marking rather than a stripe.
- **A COLLAR ROUND EACH CENTRE**, which is the reference's trumpet read at our scale. A dome sitting straight on the petals has no join, so the middle of the flower was where the eye found nothing.

**MAKING THE FLOWERS ORGANIC: four things, and the petals were not the problem.** The note was that they still read as clunky. Diagnosed close up, almost none of it was the blooms.

- **THE LEAVES WERE THE LOUDEST FAULT.** Exactly two, exactly opposite, at exactly the same height and the same angle: a bracket, not foliage, and the most mechanical thing on the plant. It is three to five now, each at its own height up the stem, its own size, and turned to its own side, so no plant has a symmetry line through it. **They follow the stem's bend** (`bend * up * up`, since the curve is quadratic) or they hang in the air beside it.
- **A CALYX, the green cup a bloom sits in.** The stem simply vanished into the petals and the head read as pushed on rather than grown. It is part of the GREENS rather than of the bloom, so it stays put while the bloom opens out of it, which is what a real one does, and it is sized from the bloom or the sunflower wears a daisy's.
- **NO TWO PETALS IN A RING ARE THE SAME.** Every petal was one geometry stamped at even angles and the eye finds that repeat immediately. `jit()` varies length, width, curl, pitch and angle by **10 percent**, seeded per flower. Ten and not twenty five because the silhouette test is the question the spike exists to answer: checked after, all eight still read apart at garden distance.
- **A GRADIENT DOWN EACH PETAL, deeper at the base and lighter at the tip**, as a vertex colour rather than a second material, so it is a MULTIPLIER over whatever hue the planter chose and the hue slider still works. A flat petal has no throat.
- **THE PLUMBING THIS NEEDED IS THE PART THAT WILL BITE.** `weldSmooth` and `mergeGeometries` both dropped everything except position and normal, so a colour attribute did not survive either. Both carry it now, and **`mergeGeometries` always emits one**, filled white where a part has none: a stem has no gradient and the leaf merged beside it does, and white is the identity under the multiply. Without that the material could not declare `vertexColors` at all.
- **A NAME COLLISION worth remembering**: `vary()` already existed for whole plant variation, and the petal helper was almost called the same thing, which would have silently replaced it. It is `jit()`.
- **BIGGER HEADS ON SHORTER STEMS**, a fifth up and a tenth down, which is the standard cute caricature. **The scale goes on an INNER group**, because `growFlower` writes the whole of the outer scale every frame and a size put there is wiped on the first frame of growth. It also helps legibility, since the bloom carries the meaning and the stem does not.

**THE PETALS ARE PILLOWS, NOT SHEETS, and the leaves got out of the way.** The reference was clay: thick inflated petals with rounded ends, small tucked leaves, a soft sheen.

- **A thin surface catches light as a thin surface**, however softly it is shaded, so no material could have got there. A petal is a closed lens now, thickest down its middle, tapering to a rounded rim all the way round. Front and back meet exactly at the boundary where the thickness is zero, so it closes with no seam to build, and `weldSmooth` averages the normals across the join so the rim shades as a curve.
- **THE RIM IS ROUND BECAUSE OF THE PROFILE.** Thickness follows `sqrt(1 - (1 - e)^2)`, a semicircle in the distance from the edge, so it arrives at the boundary PERPENDICULAR. A linear taper meets it at an angle and reads as a knife edge, and rounding that properly would have meant a separate rim strip.
- **THICKNESS IS 0.2 OF THE WIDTH AND MUST NOT GO MUCH ABOVE IT.** It was 0.42, judged against the reference's fat single petals, and that forgot the ringed blooms: a peony is 43 petals about 0.05 apart, so inflating each by 0.04 filled every gap and the flower came out **a featureless ball**. What reads as puffy on one big petal welds a ring of them into a sphere.
- **EVERY TIP IS ROUND NOW.** The width profile is evaluated at 0.9 of the way along, so it still narrows toward the tip but stops before zero: the lily still recurves and the tulip is still a closed cup, they just end in a soft cap rather than a spike.
- **LEAVES ARE HALVED AND CAPPED.** They were built from the petal routine at 0.42 of the STEM, so a poppy leaf was longer than several blooms are wide, and the sunflower's stem of 1.02 gave it leaves of 0.27 that read as paddles. They are 0.26 of the stem capped at **0.17**, rounder, and deliberately thinner than a petal: soft clay rather than fully inflated.
- **Petals carry a sheen at roughness 0.55, the green stays matte at 0.78.** A broad highlight rolling across a curve is a large part of what says the thing is solid; a shiny stem just reads as plastic.

**THEN THE FLOWERS GOT WORSE, AND THE FIX WAS SUBTRACTION.** Two passes of trying to look more organic and then more like a low poly asset pack had between them added jitter on every petal, a painted vein texture, a collar round every centre, a calyx, small leaves up the stem AND long blades from the ground, and pillow thickness on all of it. Each was defensible on its own and together they made a bed nobody could look at: the verdict was "it is not better, it became worse". **The reference wins by SUBTRACTING. Its blooms are few large regular petals in flat colour, and everything that is not the flower is quiet.** What follows is what came out, and it should be read as a warning about the next round of additions rather than as a list of settled numbers.

- **`wid` IS A HALF WIDTH, AND MISREADING IT IS WHAT CRUMPLED THE ROSE AND THE PEONY.** `petalGeometry` runs `u` from -1 to 1 and multiplies by `wid`, so a petal is about **2 * wid** across. The ring rows were written to the note "a rose petal is almost as wide as it is long" with `wid` roughly equal to `len`, which made every petal more than twice as wide as it was long. Seven of those on a ring whose widest point sits at radius 0.057 came to nearly **five times** the circumference, so no single petal was ever visible and the bloom read as crumpled foil. At about 0.55 of the length a ring overlaps twice, which is a rose wrapping itself. **Check the overlap arithmetic before adding jitter to hide a problem the numbers are causing.**
- **THE JITTER IS 0.02, DOWN FROM 0.10.** Ten percent of variation on length, width, curl, pitch and angle was put in so a ring would not read as stamped out, and on a daisy's twelve petals it did that. On a bloom of thirty three overlapping petals it destroyed the layers instead. Two percent takes the machined edge off a silhouette and cannot hide a ring.
- **THE RINGED BLOOMS ARE FEWER AND BIGGER.** The rose was five rings and thirty three petals and is four rings and twenty one; the peony was five and forty three and is three and twenty two. **The rose needs FOUR rings and the peony three, and the reason is the PITCH STEP rather than the count.** At three rings the rose stepped 18, 54, 96, and a 36 degree jump leaves a gap the next ring out cannot fill, so it came out as three flat tiers with a star on top, which is a pagoda. A rose's signature is that no one petal is fully visible, and that needs the rings close enough in pitch to bury each other.
- **THE HEART OF A RINGED BLOOM IS A CONE, NOT A SMALL FLAT RING.** At cup 0.62 the rose's inner four lay open and the middle of the flower was a hole with a star round it. At 0.84 they wrap into each other and it has a centre you cannot see into. The peony's is 0.72, looser on purpose, since its paler knot is meant to be seen.
- **RING PETALS ARE THINNER THAN SINGLE RING ONES**, `thick: 0.1` against the 0.2 default. The pillow is what stops a daisy's twelve petals reading as paper, but a ring petal passes through two or three others and every crossing of a thick one is a hard crease.
- **THE VEIN TEXTURE IS GONE.** It was a white canvas with eleven faint darker bands used as a mask, so the planter's hue still came through, and close up it did look like a petal. At the distance this is played at, eleven lines on a petal a few dozen pixels across is noise, and eight species of it is a bed that will not sit still. What is left is the base to tip vertex shade in `petalGeometry`, which says where the throat is and nothing else. `petalGeometry` still emits UVs: they cost almost nothing and a future marking, one per species rather than one for all eight, would need them.
- **THE COLLAR TORUS ROUND EVERY CENTRE IS GONE, and it was the worst of the additions.** It was meant to give the disc a rim to sit in. At garden distance a ring a shade darker than the disc, drawn round its widest part, does not read as a rim: it reads as a **dark tyre** laid on the flower, and on the daisy and the sunflower it was the first thing the eye found. The disc is a squashed sphere and already meets the petals on a curve.
- **ONE KIND OF GREEN PER PLANT.** There were two sets, small round leaves clipped up the stem and long blades from the base, so a plant carried three greens at three sizes pointing in seven directions. The reference's carry one: a few long narrow blades out of the ground, tapering to a point, and nothing on the stem. The stem leaves are deleted.
- **A BLADE MAY NOT REACH THE BLOOM, and that is a rule rather than a taste.** They were first written at up to 1.04 of the stem leaning only 10 to 26 degrees off vertical, which on a tall plant looked right and on a short one was a disaster: the daisy's heads sit at 0.27 and its blades stood 0.28 straight up around them, so the flower was **inside a cage of its own foliage and rendered in its shadow**. They are 0.48 to 0.76 of the stem at 22 to 42 degrees, which is a skirt under the head. They are also fanned by index rather than turned at random, so a clump reads as one plant's foliage.
- **THE CALYX SITS UNDER THE BLOOM, NOT IN IT.** At 0.42 of the petal length centred at `h - 0.3R` its mouth finished ABOVE the bloom's origin, so on anything that opens flat it came through the middle of the flower as a green saucer: on the lily it was the largest thing in the close up. It is 0.3 of the petal length with its mouth a hair below the origin.
- **THE POPPY CLOSED INTO A BALL and it is what `cup` does, not what `pitch` does.** Opening it by pitch alone failed twice: `cup` bends a petal UPWARD along its length, and these are long enough to reach the middle once tilted, so at pitch 56 with cup 0.44 the walls still met over the top and the poppy became a third round pink bloom beside the rose and the peony, with its one naming feature hidden. 68 with cup 0.26 is the bowl.
- **The sunflower's rays reach PAST the disc**, 0.2 against a disc radius of 0.12. At 0.16 it was mostly brown plate with a fringe round it.

**`species.html` AND `species.js` ARE THE SHEET, and they exist because six changes had landed at once and were being judged from one screenshot of the whole bed.** All eight side by side, at the distance the garden is read at and close enough to see what a head is made of, against the scene's own light. `?ids=rose,peony` narrows it, since eight columns is the right view for judging the SET and far too small for judging one bloom. It is not part of either scene and nothing imports it.

- **ONE renderer tiled with the scissor test**, not one canvas per tile: sixteen canvases is sixteen WebGL contexts and browsers cap around there.
- **It had three bugs of its own before it could be trusted, and all three looked like flower bugs.** `setSize(w, h, false)` leaves the element with no CSS width, so it lays out at its backing store size and the whole sheet renders at twice the size it is measured at. A ground circle of radius 40 with the species spaced six apart left the last two standing on the clear colour with no ground and no shadow, which read as those species being differently lit. And **forcing `receiveShadow` on every mesh made every bloom read as though it were in shade**: a petal is a thin double sided sheet, so with receive on it shadows its own back face. The kit sets cast on and receive off for that reason and the garden was right all along. **A tool built to judge the thing can be the thing that is wrong.**

**EVERY COLOUR IN `garden3d/` WAS WRONG, AND IT IS ONE ARGUMENT.** `Color.setHex` and `Color.setStyle` default their input to sRGB; **`Color.setHSL` defaults to the WORKING space**, which three sets to `srgb-linear`. So every HSL in this folder was being read as a linear value and converted up on output: `setHSL(356/360, 0.58, 0.58)` produced **#ea9da4**, a pale washed pink, where the web version's own `hsl(356,58%,58%)` is **#d2565e**. Measured against the 2D's drawing of the same flower, the 3D poppy's petals came out **17 points of saturation low**. It is not only the flowers: the grass, the sky, the three woods, the stone and the avatar all go through the same helper, which is most of what "washed out" and "out of place" were.

- **THE LIGHTING HAD QUIETLY BEEN TUNED TO COMPENSATE, so correcting the colours made the scene dark and both had to move together.** With the colours right the same petals measured lightness 37 against the 2D's 50. Exposure went 1.15 to **1.42**, the sun 2.3 to **3.0**, the hemisphere 0.5 to **0.62**, the rim 0.5 to **0.62** and `environmentIntensity` 1.05 to **1.22**, swept against the 2D drawing rather than picked. Measured after: **hue 358, saturation 63, lightness 49** against the drawing's 356, 57, 50. Saturation sits a little high on purpose, since a lit surface carries highlights a flat fill does not.
- **The diagnosis took four wrong guesses first, and each was eliminated by measurement rather than by looking**: the environment, the hemisphere light, the exposure, the tone mapper, the material's roughness and its `envMapIntensity` were all tried and none of them moved saturation more than two points. What settled it was reading `material.color.getHexString()` back and comparing it to what `hsl()` means in a browser. **When a colour is wrong, check what the colour IS before touching the light on it.**
- **`offsetHSL` at `world.js:254` is left alone.** It shifts an existing colour rather than authoring one, so it operates in the working space by design.

**THE FLOWERS ARE BEING REBUILT FROM THE 2D DRAWING FUNCTIONS, SPECIES BY SPECIES.** The kit was built from botany and a silhouette test, and the eight it produced are not the eight the web version draws. Checked against `speciesPetalCount` and the `draw*Bloom` functions:

| | the 2D draws | the 3D built |
|---|---|---|
| daisy | 16 petals, ONE head, a fixed gold centre | 12 petals, a clump of THREE heads |
| poppy | **8**, two layers of four, on and off the axes | **6**, one ring |
| peony | 10 outer alternating two lengths and tones, 6 inner, a small warm centre | 3 rings of 5/8/9, no centre at all |
| tulip | ONE connected shape, a back silhouette and a front petal | 6 separate petals in a cup |
| sunflower | a disc of **1.35 R**, wider than the petals are long | a disc of 0.12 against petals of 0.2 |
| rose | bezier petals in a spiral, ONE head | 4 rings, 21 petals, a spray of TWO heads |

A daisy with twelve petals and three heads is a different flower wearing the same name, and at garden distance that reads as wrong without being nameable. **The invented habits are going**: one bloom on one stem, as the 2D has it.

- **`sp.R` IS THE 2D DRAWING'S OWN R.** When a species carries one, every length in its rows is a multiple of it, written exactly as the web version writes them (`R * 0.675`, `R * 1.05`), so the two can be read side by side and a ratio cannot be mistranscribed. A species without an `R` is in absolute units and is untouched, which is what lets this be done one species at a time.
- **`tip: "ellipse"` is the profile most of the 2D actually uses.** Those petals are a p5 `ellipse()`, and an ellipse's width profile is `sqrt(4t(1-t))`, not a sine: at a quarter of the way along it is 0.87 of full width where a sine is 0.71. It needs no `TIPCUT`, an ellipse already ending with a vertical tangent.
- **`wid` IS A HALF WIDTH and a p5 `ellipse()` takes DIAMETERS**, so a 2D full width of `R * 0.675` is written `wid: 0.3375`. This is the same trap that crumpled the rose and the peony a session ago, from the other direction.
- **A ring may carry its own `sat` as well as its own `light`**, because the 2D rows do: the poppy's deep four are drawn at `sat * 1.05` against the bright four's plain `sat`.
- **A VERTEX SHADE MAY ONLY DARKEN, NEVER LIGHTEN.** It ran 0.84 to **1.14**, and a multiplier above one pushes a colour toward white, which desaturates it. It is 0.80 to 1.00, so the species' declared colour IS the colour of the brightest part of the petal. Same rule the vein texture mask followed.
- **`COLS` is 6, not 4.** Five points across a petal renders an elliptical end as a blunt polygon, which is visible the moment the outline is put beside the drawing it copies.

**THE POPPY IS THE FIRST ONE DONE, transcribed rather than designed.** Eight petals in two layers of four, `addRings` supplying the 45 degree offset for free since it already turns an odd ring by half a step and half of four is 45. The near black eye is the 2D's `fill(hue, 34, 13)`, with its three ribs and its six dots.

- **PITCH AND CUP ARE THE ONLY THINGS NOT IN THE 2D**, and they cannot be avoided: a flat drawing says nothing about either, and at pitch 90 with no cup this is a dinner plate seen from a camera 42 degrees up. The deep four lie flatter and lower and the bright four stand over them, which is the 3D reading of the 2D's draw order.
- **A CENTRE IS MATTE, ROUGHNESS 0.95, against the petals' 0.55.** The eye came out mid brown and the cause was the sheen rather than the colour: a glossy dome rolls a broad highlight straight across the one feature that names the species.
- **`discGeometry`'s HALF HEIGHT IS `h`, NOT `h / 2`.** It builds a sphere of radius r and scales y by `h / r`, so its extent is plus and minus h. Reading it as half put all three ribs inside the eye, where they rendered perfectly and could not be seen.
- **The dots sit on the PETALS, not on the eye**, at 0.2875 against the eye's own 0.21, which is what the 2D draws, so their height has to clear the petal surface at that radius or they are buried in it.

**THE SHEET SHOWS THE 2D DRAWING IN A THIRD ROW, drawn by the web version's own code.** `species.html` loads `bouquet/bouquet-sketch.js` and calls its global `drawSpeciesBloom(ctx, species, R, hue, sat, light)`, which takes a raw Canvas 2D context and needs no p5. `boot()` returns immediately when `#bqBuilder` is absent, the same guard that lets the landing page load that file. So "is this a 3D version of our 2D flower" is a question the sheet ANSWERS rather than one anybody has to hold in their head, and it cannot drift from what the site draws.

- **`preserveDrawingBuffer: true` on the sheet's renderer**, so it can be measured and not only looked at. Without it the WebGL canvas reads back empty once the frame is composited and a pixel sample reports nothing, which looks exactly like a render that failed.
- **`window.__sheet` hands out the scene, the renderer and `draw`**, so a lighting or material change can be swept against the drawing beside it without a file edit per experiment. That is how the four wrong guesses above were eliminated in one pass.

**THE PIXEL EXPERIMENT, on a button rather than as a decision.** It is OFF by default now: `PIXEL_SCALE` is 1 in `world.js` and the spike calls `setPixelScale` at boot, because the module default and the button's starting index are two different things and letting them disagree left the canvas low resolution with the button claiming otherwise. The question was whether going pixel art like Stardew would be better and easier. It is on `PIXEL_SCALE`, cycled by the Pixels button in the spike, and it is forty lines that can be deleted in one.

- **THE PIXEL LOOK IS A SIZE, NOT A SHADER.** `sizeRenderer` sets the canvas's BACKING STORE to the real size divided by the scale and lets CSS stretch that small image over the full element with `image-rendering: pixelated`. So the scene is genuinely rendered at a low resolution and blown up, the way a pixel game is, rather than being a filter over a sharp render. At scale 4 a 888 by 987 element is drawn at 222 by 247.
- **A RENDER TARGET AND A BLIT SHADER IS THE OBVIOUS WAY AND IT IS WORSE HERE.** Tone mapping and the sRGB conversion are applied by three when it draws to the CANVAS, so rendering to a target and copying it across means doing that conversion twice or not at all, and both look wrong. Sizing the canvas changes nothing about how a frame is produced, only how big it is. It also costs LESS to run: a quarter scale frame is a sixteenth of the pixels.
- **THE FINDING: it flatters the world and costs the FLOWERS.** Close up the blooms read beautifully, crisp pixel edges on clean colour. At the garden camera a bloom is about 40 screen pixels, which at scale 4 is **ten pixels across**, and ten pixels is not enough for a generated bloom to keep its species. Stardew's flowers are that size too and they work because they are DRAWN for that grid; downsampling a 3D bloom gets the texture of pixel art without its legibility.
- **2 IS THE SETTLED SCALE, compared side by side at the garden camera** by running two live copies of the page stacked in iframes, which is the only way to judge it without remembering the other one. At 2 every bloom keeps its shape and the fence rails and path stones hold; at 3 the lavender spike and the daisy go mushy, the rails break up and neighbouring blooms begin merging. 4 and 6 stay on the button to see the look pushed, not to use.
- **Which is the honest answer to "easier".** A pixel garden needs art authored for the pixel grid, roughly a hundred sprites across eight species, three growth stages, tiles, props and an avatar, plus indexed palettes to keep the planter's chosen hue working. That is not easier, it is differently hard, and it trades a generative system for an illustration one.

**TWO SHADING LANGUAGES ON PURPOSE: FACETED SCENERY, SMOOTH FLOWERS.** The reference for this pass was a low poly farm scene, and the thing to notice about it is that it is NOT uniformly smooth: its trees and rocks are hard edged planes while its characters are soft blobs. That mix is deliberate here too.

- **`flatShading: true` is back on every material in `world.js`**, which is all scenery and terrain, and stays OFF in `flower-kit.js` and `avatar-kit.js`. So the crisp chunky scenery and the rounded petals from the previous pass both survive. **If a new material is added, the file it lives in decides which language it speaks.**
- **The rocks are faceted icosahedra again**, jittered so no two are the same lump, and TERRACOTTA rather than grey: grey stone reads as a quarry and pulls the whole palette cold. `stoneMat` takes a `warm` flag and the shrubs went back to icosahedra with it.
- **THE LAND IS NEARLY FLAT NOW, a tenth of its old amplitude.** The reference's field is one clean plane of lime with the scenery standing on it, and rolling ground fights that: every dip put a gradient across ground that wants to read as a single colour. The island and its soil edge stay, so there is still a horizon and still somewhere the garden ends.

**THE PLANTING IS A RAISED BED, NOT A PAINTED RECTANGLE.** It was a flat slab: a brown rectangle lying on grass with no thickness and no edge.

- **`buildRaisedBed` makes a box out of BOARDS, and being able to see the boards is the whole of it.** Every wall is two stacked planks with a gap between them rather than one tall panel, there is a post at each corner, and the soil is sunk 5cm below the rim so the boards contain it.
- **It returns its soil mesh and its top height**, because the sketch raycasts that mesh to decide where a flower may go and plants at that height. `bed` in `spike.js` is now that soil mesh, so every existing rule about the bed still reads correctly.
- **`buildFence` runs along the BACK only.** Fencing two sides, as the reference does, boxes the planting in from this camera and puts rails across the flowers.

**THE PUMP IS THE ONLY SATURATED ACCENT, and that is why the eye lands on the bed.** In a green and brown picture one red object does all the directing, so nothing else should take that job. `buildPump` and `buildBucket` stand at the near end of the bed; at the far end the bed hid everything but the handle.

**`buildConeTree` RINGS BOTH SCENES.** Three stacked cones on a short trunk, each narrower and a shade lighter, at seven radial segments so the facets are big enough to read as planes. **The tiers are what make it a tree rather than a bush**: one cone is a party hat, and the step between them is what says branches. They sit outside the planting circle in both scenes, so nothing anybody grows is ever behind one.

**THE LOOK: A COSY ISLAND, LATE AFTERNOON, AND A SLOWER FLOWER.** `world.js` holds the light, the sky, the ground and the life, and both scenes call `dressScene`, for the same reason `growth.js` exists: two copies of a look drift inside a session.

- **A FLOWER TAKES AS LONG AS ITS SIZE SAYS IT SHOULD.** `SPEED` is 2.6 against the web version's pace and `PACE` is each species against the others, multiplied rather than replaced so the SHAPE of the 2D sequence is kept and only its length changes. Measured: daisy 7.1s, lily 8.8s, sunflower 11.1s, sakura 16.8s. Watching a tree reach full height in the time a daisy takes was the single thing that most said "animation". **This deliberately breaks the match with the web version's 3400ms**, which only matters if the two are ever shown side by side.
- **THE COEFFICIENT IS NOT THE OVERSHOOT.** The bloom now settles rather than stopping dead, through a back-out ease. At 0.3 the peak measured **1.002**, two parts in a thousand and invisible; at 1.2 it is **1.053**, which reads as a bloom settling. Measure the peak, never read it off the constant.
- **AN ISLAND, NOT A PLAIN, and this was the fix for the whole look.** At this camera angle a ground plane that runs past the frame fills every pixel, so the graded sky was never once on screen and the hills read as slabs lying on a lawn. A finite island has an EDGE and past the edge is sky. It is also the reference the direction came from, which the first attempt had quietly dropped in favour of a field. The island is **1.7 times the plot**: at 2.1 it was 18 units across against a frame that sees about 15, and it still filled everything.
- **The hills sit ON THE RIM and they are small.** Mounds at two thirds of the island turned the whole thing into a crater with a garden at the bottom of it.
- **The camera pitch is 0.72, and it is a compromise measured both ways.** At the old 0.82 there is no horizon in frame at all. At 0.62 the sky and the far rim are both visible, but a daisy and a sunflower are flat faced blooms and at that angle they read as thin ellipses. 0.72 keeps the horizon and keeps the faces.
- **The fog starts BEYOND the garden.** The camera stands sixteen units out, so a near plane of 16 laid haze over the flowers themselves and washed the whole scene grey. It is 23 to 41, which leaves it on the rim and the hills where it belongs.
- **The land's displacement has to be SHALLOW.** Every facet it tilts turns away from a low sun, so a landscape that looks gentle from above goes muddy at this angle. Half the amplitude over twice the wavelength.
- **The plot's grass is the SAME tone as the land around it.** A lighter disc reads as pasted on; what marks the garden is what is planted on it.
- **Tufts are what stop the ground reading as painted lino**: three flat blades each, ninety of them, merged into one mesh so they cost one draw call.
- **The life follows `garden-life.js`'s rules rather than reinventing them**: motes drifting slowly upward, three butterflies on two sines of unrelated periods whose wings never shut completely, and one skein of birds that crosses for thirty seconds and then leaves the sky alone.
- **The music is the project's own engine**, `../garden-music.js` with `data-mood`, calm on the plot and bright on the shared day. It builds its own button at top right, so the legend card moved down to 70 to clear it.
- **A silent no-op worth remembering**: a scripted replace whose anchor no longer matches changes nothing and still reports success. The camera width edit did exactly that and the view stayed where it was through a whole round of looking. **Check the value in the file afterwards, not the script's exit.**

## Conventions

- Indentation is inconsistent (the shared sketch is largely unindented at top level). Match the surrounding block rather than reformatting; whole-file reformatting would bury real diffs.
- Colors are HSL throughout the sketches. Flowers carry `hue` (user-chosen), plus `sat`/`light` from `defaultSat(species)`/`defaultLight(species)`. Petal shape ratios live in `speciesShapeCfg()`.
- p5 sketches use `angleMode(DEGREES)` and `noiseSeed(9999)`; keep deterministic seeding when adding generative detail.
- The vendored `*-p5.min.js` and `*-p5.party.js` are minified third-party bundles, never edit them.
